' is a strange fiict, but true, that although Mr do n Morley is one of our best-known historical an philosophical writers, and though he !lOW fills farl the second time the office of Chief Secretary for Ire and, it is only recently that a set of his books ha e been added to the library in Dublin Castle for th use of Government officials. ' including the Lo d Lieutenant ,and the Chief Secretary. . The nucleus of the library was the property originally of the Irish House of Lords, and was transferred to Duin Castle at the time of the Union. For many years Parliament has voted an annual grant to keep the library up to date by the addition of works of reference and the best books of current literature. • = . Ihe late Professor Seeley's " Ecce nomo," pub- Bs ed in 1868, occasioned as will be remembered; a hot controversy, to which the principal contributor waS Mr. Gladstone. In his criticism the latter, while not by any •means in entire agreement with Professor .Seeley, wrote that the . Look was" a powerful specimen of his handiwork," and went on as follows—" It is doubtless needful that popular th logy, whichelike everything else,tends to gettle don into mere-formulas, should Otis be shaken up from time to time, and measured and adjusted , by ts eternal standard : that we may • come at last neer to a sense bow truly the treasure is divine which is lodged unworthily in us poor 03(irtheA,4 vessels." , ------ f ~ 1 The story goes in Dublin that the Chief Secretary the Lord Lieutenant, and others, one eveiing, after dinner at the Castle, had a conversation in which refrence was made to a particular passage' in'Mr,' Mofrley'S- book on " Compromise " The- drift 'or. Menning of the passage was disputed, and Lord Ilciughton sent for -tothe-library ; but to the astonishment of his Excellency and everyone else present—save, pehaps, the author—the book -Was notto be , found there. What is more, not a single one of Mr. Morley's works was in the collec- tion: -It-seems th-at-t-trey-had-all-been-placed on-ttre- " Index Expurgatorius " of the permanent officials of the Castle. However, by order of the Lord Lieu- ' tenant the complete series has now been added. .

There are some startling statements Contained in an interview which a representative of the "Pall Mall Budget" has had with M. Himon, the author of " France Societe et Politique," a gentle- man of standing and authority, now living'in Lon- don. M. Hereon said:—" I can now give you a hint of a scandal very soon to burst forth that will make those affairs shrink into insignificance. The affair of Pertain' again. The 'Grand Francais 'is dead, and his son, Charles, de Lesseps, and M. Marius Fontenot are going td, speak ! They hive _suffered in silence a long time, because a threat was held over their heads that if they committed indiscretions of speech the Vieux Comte would be thrown into prison ! Now he is gone, and a verit- able social bomb will soon explode.''

The return of Ole Postmaster-General from Monte Carlo to attend the Cabinet Council gate the alarm- ists a" reason?' for their prophecies of dissolution and what not. But those who attach political im- portance to a journey taken—by the General for for the purpose of being present at a meet- ing of his colleagues are ;astoundingly ignorant of the character of Mr. Arnold Morley. If be had been in Constantinople be would 'have returned all the same if there had been time for that operation between the receipt of the summons and the meet- ing of the Cabinet. The Postmaster-General never misses anything—a division, a meeting of the Cabinet,or whatever itrmay be. He is a former chief Whip, and he has all the instincts of a disciplina- rian-- Be has been-lcuown to_leave a dinner patty at his own residence for the purpose of helping to' make a House at nine o'clock, though he knew well enough that there would be a count out a few minutes afterwards.

-

The name of Mr. Thomas Andrews, of Guildford whose death is just announced, was not. so widely known to the general public as was that of the naturalist Bnckland, but itt was known to every angler who took an enlightened "'interest in his sport and to every breeder of fish throughout the empire. Many of the Australasian streams have been stocked with salmon and other fish from the eggs which he cultivated and exported. He was the most extensive bfeeder of fish in England, and I perhaps even in the world. To him it was an en- thusiasm as well as a profitable business, and his beautiful fish farm, if I may so call his lovely end interesting ponds at Haslemere, were the most striking points of attraction on that famous com- mon. He was an importer of fish as well as an ex- porter, and his imports were more from his love of the occupation than for gain. Rare fishes from Tnd ia.Chin r, Buenos Ayres, and other distant places found their way to his pools. He published many papers on matters relating to his Ailing, and his bright, cheery presence will he missed by all interested in the occupation he followed. •

Since I am alluding to fishery matters, I observed that the latest form of league which has been pro- posed is a "Salmon League." It is a still younger idea than the Sportinv League. One of its objects is to enlist the co-operation of riparian owners and Proprietors and lessees of salmon waters in tracing the natural history of the salmon. The assistance of the catchers of the Ash will also be indispensable and it is thought that this can be secured by pay- ment of fees orrewards. Some valuable discoveries in the movements of the fish are expected to be made, hut the co-operation would require to be very wide if much is to result from it.

While the Radical party in the House will loyally support the policy propounded last year, they will not submit to anemic stricken shipbuild- ing scheme which would deprive the poorer classes of the cominnnity of all the benefits of last year's Budget. You will not meet a Radical in the National Liberal Club who does not frankly own that it would be as much as his seat was worth if he appealed to the Radicals of his constituency for their suffrages without being able to announce to them a substantial reduction in duty in some of their articles of daily consumption, such as tea or tobacco. A reduction on the duty of the latter would be appreciated by most working-men. The pettiest to.bacconist in London could give Sir William more light on the operation of the tobacco duty on the humblest classes of the consumers than any great firm in the kingdOm but, whether the abatement is on tobacco or tea or anything else,he will have to satisfy the widespread feeling that a large share of the improved financial nositien must he '(levoted to a remission of the indirect burdens which still weigh upon the-,poor. •

I will work for you and be your servant. And will you forgive me, and take me back ? " ~. Peggy's honest face glowed with a great joy. Her rugged features were transfigured with the love that so long repressed now leaped up in a full tide toward her repentant lover. She had loved him, and waited for him eighteen years, and this moment recompensed her-for all- her anxieties, tears, and desolation. _She bent over. him, took hit hand in hers._ansl._ kissed him as shyly as a young girl might have done. . And then she drew back blttshing and embar- , reseed. Hers was not a demonstrative nature, and, thik was the first kiss she had given since 'Negwyn ta4 left her. ‘1 Oh, Peggy !" cried" her lover, not daring to a7pt his good fortune. "What does this mean ?" It intani,Giterge; that we begin now 'vi .w left off eighteen years ago," answered the 'wo an, in her calm, quiet voice, that soothed him an strengthened him like a bracing breeze. • "It m$ ti ns that-you Must hurry and get , well, that- "We maiv be married." Negwyn's delight was equalled only by his sa- toi ishment. That this good, self-reliant, honour- ab woman should be willing to, accept him as ie he husband, after all that had passed, seemed in- credible. He began to fear that he Ethenicl-wake- upisnd find it all a dreban. ' eggy brought a chair and sat beside his benl,4 holing his hand. They talked a while of the pail* and Negwyn had grown quite calm, when the !woman said: . ‘,‘ You begin to look strong and well, George. I thOak, with proper care, you could make a journey toAday." 1‘ A journey? You are not going to send me viclay 1" j‘ No, I shall go with you!" 4,` But where ?' he asked, wonderingly. 1‘ George," said the woman solemnly, " Provi- since has sent me back my lover alive and soon to iiwell. But there is a noble lady whose lover 0 supposed to be lost to her eighteen years ago, U who has carried all these years a heavy load of lef. You know who I mean—the Lady Beatrice mpton." r Yes, I know," said Negwyn uneasily. 1' All these years the Lady Beatrice has been ieretly a wife, secretly a mother. Her husband 3 poor Mr. Geoffrey TreVelyan, who dares not show head because of the charge of attempted murder -it him. He is hiding to-day, as he has been since the night on which he fled from Treve- c Park. You can clear his name, George, and . must do it. Not an hour must be lost. Mrs. impton told me that the marquis and Lord , lowe were in pursuit of him.' IThe Lady; trice is at Hamptonwold. You and I must set for Hamptonwold to-day." Eer clear eyes looked straight into the shrinking (era soul before her. Her 'manner, firm and ,tong, nerved him. He made no denial, no ,irestations, but resigned- himself quietly to her w 11. 'But how shall I explain, this long silence of yelars ?" he asked. "The Lady Beatrice and Mr. thloffrey will never forgive me.' Do right' George, and let the result take care of!itself," said the brave Peggy. "And now let us see how strong you are.— You must dress." She brought him his garments and helped him to p t them on. Then nhe bathed his face and ' fiide, combed out his hair and bear S; and as ted him into the easiest chair the house af- foided. --. i 7 r There! You look better 1" ehetaclaimed, look- ing at him with the conscious pride of proprietor- shy. "You will take your broth and some wine, and we Will then discuss this journey." She brought him the bowl of broth, and fed him with a spoon as if he hail been ca chilld. Then, hiving given him the wine, and witnessed a great ireprovement in him, she announced that he had strength enough to undertake the journey. "I suppose," ventured Negwyn, hesitatingly, . "that we couldn't wait till I get well 2". ," Wait! when his enerniet are in pursuit of Mr. Geoffrey; when the family is scattered, and the poor Lady Beatrice is suffering worse than you can suffer ?" cried Peggy, reproachfully.. "Why, George !" Negwyn apologized at once, expressing a desire to depart on the instant. Peggy, well pleased with his submission, went to the door and summoned her shepherd assistant, who. came running to meet her. "Take the donkey and ride over to Dalton, lad," she said, briskly, "and bring back a pair of the best horses and a large invalid carriage. Mr. Negwyn is going off on a long journey. Be quick, now, and don't let the grass grow under the donkey's feet. And don't haggle about the price of the horses, and be sure to get a skilful driver." Thus commissioned, the shepherd lad set off on his errand, and Peggy returned to her lover. It was nearly three hours before the carriage made its appearance, and during that period Peggy made her own travelling toilet, collected bottles of wine and beef-tea and pillows, and made every preparation for the comfort of the invalid. The carriage, however, came at last, the shepherd lad on the box with the driver, the donkey attached by a rope to the rear of the vehicle. Peggy placed her house and flock in the lad's charge, with many directions, and then proceeded to make a comfortable couch within the vehicle, piling blankets and pillows 'Thickly. The invalid was brought out by the coachman and the shepherd lad, and laid carefully on his new bed. His bottles of food and drink were packed in carefully among the folds of woollen, and Peggy locked her door, giving the key to her faithful young assistant, and entered the carriage, giving her destination to the driver, with many injunctions to drive alowly and carefully. And thus they set out on their important pil- grimage. The ride over the Burnt Downs, as careful as the driver might be, could not fail to cause Negwyn great physical pain, but he bore it bravely, finding strength and consolation in the firm clasp of Peggy's hands, and her frequent encouraging allu- sions to their future. Their progress was necessarily slow. They drove hour after hour, leaving the downs behind them, and entering upon pleasant country roads, where the motion of the carriage was comparatively easy to bear. Peggy bestowed constant attention upon her lover, giving him such food as he could bear, and frequently felt of his pulse, which to her secret alarm, beg -n to quicken. The journey was two-thirds done when the hot flush on Negwyn's face, and the burning heat of his skin, assured his nurse that he was threatened with a relapse into that fever from which he had already experienced so narrow an escape. Of course, further journeying would but aggravate these symptoms. Peggy was therefore compelled to halt at the first village at which they arrived after her discovery, and to put up at the inn. Negwyn was got to bed, a physician was Eent for, and the task of subduing the incipient fever was again entered upon. Peggy's heart fell at this de- lay, and anxieties for the Lady Beatrice mingled with those that assailed her on account of her lover. She felt a keen foreboding that the pursuit of Geoffrey Trevelyan was drawing to a close— that a crisis in his fate was near—and an awful fear rent her soul that the only thing that could benefit hlin—Negwyn'a evidence—would arrive too late.

Your fractures have not been SO serious as was at first supposed. In short, ‘lyou canalt uP to-day. It will not be long before,: you can go back again to the place you came from." Negwyn looked around the pleasant little chamber with a sigh—looked up at the honest face of his hostess with a wistful gaze—and then his hand fluttered uprseeking-herown brown, hard hand, which still lay on his pillow. "Peggy I" he said, in a whisper. The woman started, and her lip quivered but she made no verbal answer. "Peggy !" he said again, pleadingly, all the sinister hardness and coarseness that had been the growth of years leaving his face before the force of a genuine and softening emotion. "You know me, Peggy—George Negwyn 1" ' I knew you from the first, George," replied Peggy, quietly, Withdrawing her hand. Negwyn's countenance fell. "You hate me, I suppose, Peggy?" he questioned, eagerly. , , No. I hate no one," she answered, coolly. Negwyn was silent a moment, studying her quiet unmoved face. Old memories were strug- gling within him. His old affection for her woke to renewed life. She seemed so calm and strong and self-reliant, and he was weak and yielding, and needed a support. He asked himself if it were likely that she would ever look kindly upon him again. "Peggy," he said again, abruptly, "we were promised lovers once, were we not 1" _ "Yes," answered Peggy. "I gave you my promise to marry you only a short time before you went away so secretly. You were ,long gone, George—eighteen years. Negwyn winced at this reminder of his base- ness. "You loved me then, Peggy—eighteen years ago," he said, in a trembling voice. "I went away because—because I could not meet your honest face. I was hired to go away. But all these years, Peggy, I have thought of you and loved you. Many a night in Australia I have lain awake thinking of you. Many's the time that the tears came with the thought of you. If I'd stuck to you, Peggy, I shouldn't have been where I am to-day." Peggy seemed moved at this appeal, but made no answer. "I was on my way to you when I got this hurt," continued Negwyn. " Wigby had told me where you lived, and that you wasn't married; and a yearning took the to see you once more. And you took me in, Peggy, and took care of me, and I owe icy life to You. . "I should have done as much for any one else," observed Peggy, looking intently out of the win- dow. "I suppose so," returned Negwyn, choking back a sob. "I know I can't be anything to you now, Peggy. No woman would forgive eighteen years' neglect. But, Peggy, the sight of you has brought back the old love stronger than ever. I must tell- you, even if you put me out for it. The sight of - you, so good and calm and honest, brings back the,,, days when I was more worthy of you—when I too was honest. You couldn't forgive me, could you, Peggy!" he concluded, humbly. "There's a great deal to forgive," observed Peggy, meditatively; but her brown cheek began to flush slowly, and the old love that had never died out in her heart began to stir strongly within her. "I know there is," assented Negwyn, with in- oreasinghilinility. "There is' too much—l don't suppose you can forgive me," and the tears dropped like rain from his eyes. Peggy's hand fluttered a little, as if she would have clasped his. She restrained the impulse, and

A 'COOPERATIVE COLLIERY IN WEST

CUMBERLAND.

Mrs. Melrose, wife of the vicar of the parish, cut the first sod of • the shaft of a new colliery at Durham on Tuesday, which she christened Crossbow Colliery. Some months ago, when the Lonsdale pit was closed, the people of the large and populous village of Dearborn were left practically workless. The villagers attempted to float a company to continua working the coal deposits, and being supported financially by some sympathising men of business in the district, were successful. The, villa gem invested their savings in the venture, and th(ft local Go-operative Society advanced £1,000; and a 4 aim ast,every worker at the pit will be a shareholder', it will be practically a co-operative colliery.

MY DEAREST NELLIE, I am going to give you some more hints about etnWg dresses, which everyone wants just at this bfte of the year. The consciousness of wearing a pretty and becoming dress adds not a little to our enjoyment of dances and other Christmas gaieties. No doubt, the majority of us have made our last season's frocks do duty for pre-Christmas festivi- ties, and we are, therefore, now able to inveraB new and smart attirb to do justice to ourselv vlt the seas-a7Tirelafowing are eliarming dresses. 1 e first is a handsome dress of sapphire-blue lvetaen, trimmed with jet, the latter fcrming a inted corslet, and deep, open work square at the neck. A graceful little frock in apple-green nun's v iling, with two rows of pink satin ribbon edging • t o bottom of the full skirt, is another one. The sleeves to the elbow are very gracefully arranged, 1 a d a fold of pink crepe de Chine crosses the b dice, and is finished oil with a paste buckle and f 1 loop bows on the left side. must not forget a very handsome ball dress of s in. It is made of dead white satin covered th white crêpe line, ' the 'fall bodice being c rmingly.arranged. with a deep 'folded, band. of 7 e pink satin and skays of mush roses and fib- ) appearing on the shoulders.. : • 17

For a girl of from 14 to 16 years of age nothing could be more graceful than the evening bodice., I arb going to describe. It is made of nun's cloth, trimmed with swansdown, or feather ruching may be used. The bodice is a round waist shape, and is suitable to wear with any of the fashionable _ skirts. The foundation of the front is trimmed with an overparb pleated in V shape from the shoulder seam to the bust, and is completed from here with an overpart scalloped at the top and edged with swansdown, the back being also finished in a similar manner, and is fastened with buttons in the centre. The neck in front is out low V shape, and it is edged round with swansdown edging. The flowing sleeves to look well should be lined with sa.rcenet. They are bordered with swansdown, and are gathered into the under part of the armhole, an epaulette of swansdown meeting them on the shoulders. Pink gauze too, makes a suitable bodice blouse for even• ing wear.

A saw bonnet—the butterfly--Is fashioned in velvet and irridesoent beads. The shape is flat, oovered in royal blue mit oir velvet, with a double butterfly bow made of blue iridescent beads on either side. The sane idea may be successfully carried out in a Trieter colour by those who con. eider some of the present combinaticns of colours and materials too daring and original to indulge in. A suitable toque for ordinary wear is made of velvet and fur. The crown of mouse-brown velvet is slightly full, and contrasts prettily with the border of squirrel tails, one of which is twisted at the book to form an erect bow, with folds of velvet. IA hats this is rather a fashionable shape. It is of brown felt, with a pointed witch's crown somewhat low, with wings on either side of brown and gold, and a steel buckle clasping black bows at the front and back.

•A charming collar with yoke, for quiet evening wear, is of yellow chiffon. The sateen or silk yoke foundation is covered with a full round bertha gathered at the neck and lower edge, outlined with a double frill of chiffon, which is gathered with a small heading, then secured to the edge of yoke. A drawn collar is arranged over the plain founda- tion by a tuck being made in the material each side of the centre of front, whit& is drawn up to the size of foundation, then secured to it. Each end at the centre of back is finist.ed in the same manner, where it fastens with books and loops. A very ,pretty aollarette, seen in Regent-street the other day, was of butter-oolonred satin ribbon, about an inch and a half wide, made to hook at the back, under smart, stand-out bows, that showed in front. Al the two sides, under the ears the ribbon was set in double loops, like puffs, while in the middle was a paste buokle. Black satin ribbon is rnuoh used, made as just described, but with the addition of two rosettes of pretty-ooloured chiffon placed in front, with about three inches between them. A supply of these rosettes, in different tints, will enable the black collarette—which may or may nob hays& fall of lace added—to go with many different gowns. Yours lovingly, J

" GOTHENBURGISII."

WITH A DIFFERENCE. SG much has been heard from temperance reformers and politicians about the various methods of dealing with the drink question that Much interest will be felt in the action that is being taken in Aberdeen. The attention which has been given to the question of the public control of the liquor traffic in that town has resulted in the drawing up of a Bill which it is intended to submit to Parliament, if the general community will support the scheme. The intention of the promoters of the Bill is to obtain for the municipality the control of the liquor traffic in the town. The system differs largely from that which has been tried in Gothenburg. Briefly, at the end of five years from the passing of the Act the whole of the public-houses will be controllal by the municipality. During the five years no new licenses are to be granted except in favour of the municipality, which has the power to buy out existing licence-holders, and also to employ them as retailers. On to the scheme is grafted an ingenious system of local veto, whereby at the end of every five years a poll would be taken of the inhabitants. Two-thirds of those voting would decide whether the sale of drink was to be continued. Should the decision be against the further sale all the public- houses would be closed until the inhabitants had declared 13.7 their votes at one of the quinquennial ballots that they should be re-opened. The profits after deduct- ing cert Ain charges, are to be devoted to any objects which the Town Council may decide upon. It remains to be seen whether Parliament will sanction the Bill to carry out this scheme.

WARRINGTON TECHNICAL

INSTITUTE.

ANNUAL PRIZE DISTRIBUTION.

There was a fair attendance at the Museum Lecture Room, on Monday evening, when the Mayor (Councillor James Fairclough) distributed the prizes and certificates , gained by the scholars of the Warrington Municipal Technical Institute Classes, during the past session. Mr. F. Monks, J.P., C.C., occupied the chair, and at the commencement said the numbers of students had increased,and the classes which failed last year had been successful in. the last examinations. He only regretted that the young people in the town did not take more advantage of the opportunity afforded them by the pro- vision of certain studies by the committee. He thought it would be little less than folly to continue paying for teachers unless there was a sufficient number of students, and he ventured to say that if there was a considerable number of students in the town who wished for any particular subject to be taught in the Institute, the committee were quite willing to consider any proposals with a view to meet the demand. With reference to the £6O scholarship given by the Lancashire County Council, he was glad to say that one of the School of Art students from Wigan—Mr. Millard—had been suc- cessful in winning it, being the fourth in the Warrington School of Art since the first offering of prizes. (Applause.) The students who had gone to Paris had done very well and he understood that Mr. Millard had also gone to Paris or would proceed there immediately. The SECRETARY (Mr. Madeley) in reading THE REPORT, said " In presenting a report on its third year's work. the Committee has again to record a material enlargement of its sphere of influence, and a satisfactory record of work done. A change in the regulations of the Educa- tion Department made it practicable for the Committee to undertake the management of the Evening Continua- tion School, which had been established by a voluntary committee in 1892, and the Technical Institute has now assunaed proportions which may be considered fairly complete, so far as organization is concerned. The total number of students who joined the schools during the session was 733, composed as follows :- 1885. 1893. 1892. Pchool of Ar+ • 157 . 160 School of Science CO atinuation School

733

Of these students, 12 were entrance sehgilars from the elementary day schools of the town, selected on the result of an examination held for the purpose, and 59 held scholarships granted by the Committee of the Institute on the result of the examinations at the end of the previous session. In addition, the fees of about 100 students were paid, wholly or in part, by the Warring- ton Co-operative Society. In all cases the assistance granted was conditional on regular attendance at the classes. For the instruction of this body of pupils some 92 classes were held weekly, under the charge of 30 teachers and assistants. The Committee-desires to re- cord the receipt from the Lancashire County Council of a special grant of £l5O for the purchase of apparatus, and from the Cheshire County Council the continuance of their capitation grant on Cheshire students attending the Institute. For 1893-4 the amount of this grant was £2B 10s. The sum of £2 2s. sd. was received from the former Committee of the Continuation School as a dona- tion towards a prize fund. The Committee is indebted to the Co-oberative Society for the loan of tables, and to Mr. F. W. Monks for his kindness in providing an oxy- hydrogen lantern for lectures on several occasions. The Committee desires also to record its satisfaction at the continued good service rendered by Air. Aylward, and the other teachers of the Institute.

SCHOOL OF SCIENCE, TECHNOLOGY, AND CO7,I3IFIRCE. In the syllabus of the third session of the School of Science the changes were very slight. The only quite new subject introduced was music, class being suc- cessfully formed for the teaching of tlia theory of music and singing. A slaort course of lecture on analysis was delivered to the students of practical °chemistry ; and the arithmetic class was arranged sn as to give more prominence to technical arithmetic and mensuration. All the other classes of the previous session remained on the programme, with the exception of cookery, which

was transferred to the Continuation School. Two classes (Physics and Mathematics : Stage IL) were dis- continued for want of a sufficient number of entries. For the commercial examinations of the Union of Lanca- shire and Cheshire Institutes there was a greater num- ber of entries, and a larger proportion of passes, than last year. In the comnaerci rl examinations the number of papers worked was. 1894, 170 ; 1893, 108 ' • 1892, 186 ; number of passes, 1894, 88; 1893, 73 ; and 1892, 111, respec- tively. In the science examinations of the Science and Art Department the results were variable, the general effect being that in the advanced stage candidates did better, but in the elementary stage worse, than last year. Compared with a number of other schools in this dis- trict, our elementary candidates just reached the aver- age, whilst the advanced candidates were above it. The science examinations showed the number of papers worked as, 1894, 174 ; 1893. 168 ; number of passes, 1894, 71 ; and 1893, 82 respectively. Four members of the iron and steel class sat for the examination of the City and Guilds of London Institute, when three were successful in passing in the ordinary grade, one of whom (Henry Hope) was awarded the bronze medal of the Institute and the third prize (value 20s. given by the Ironmongers' Company. The om-c mittee has pleasure in recording the continued sucessc of this, which is so far the only class which it has been found practicable to establish in connection with any specially local industry. Under the rules laid down by the committee each first class obtained at the examinations by a student of the School of Science entitles the holder to a scholar- ship, value ss. to 155.. for the elementary or advanced stage respectively. Sixty-two scholarships were in con- sequence awarded on the result of the examination being known. Summer Term.—A third term was pro- vided as before for the dressmaking class, and also far the Spanish and French classes. CONTINUATION SCHOOL. It arranging the syllabus of the Continuation School for the Session, several new subjects were added, with the view of providing more effectively for that one of the objects of the school, which consists in preparing students for the School of Science. The most important addition was a class in'the Elements of Chemistry, which was held in the lecture-room at the School of Science, and which was a very popular class. A class in Theoretical Mechanics was also provided, but it was announced as an afterthought in place of geo- graphy, for which there were no entries, and so its numbers remained small. Cookery was given as an or- dinary for girls, with only moderate success as regards entries, although, as usual with this subject, most of those who did join became very much interested in the work. The number of students who joined during the session was 197, as against 146 the previous year. To encourage regular attendance the Committee offered prizes of books of certain values, the selection being made by the scholars, each for himself, to all who made a certain number of attendances. Fifty pupils qualified for these prizes, which were presented to the successful candidates at the commencement of the present session by Mr. Thomas Morris, at the request of this Commit- tee. A number of the students entered for the Elemen- tary Examination of the Union of Lancashire and Che- shire Institutes,and on the results of these examinations Institute Scholarships were given as follows :—One Scholarship, value 10s., tenable at School of Science : Mary Carpenter. Ten Scholarships, value 7s. 6d., ten- able at Continuation School, with one class in School of Science: T. Longshaw, W. Parker, Susannah Penny, Sarah Horobin, Edith Gill, T. Gandy, U. Gandy, H. Greene, Florence M'Garty, J, Barton. Two Scholarships, value 55., tenable in Continuation School : Annie Bas- ketter, Ella London. On the conclusion of the Session, the report received from the Education Department on the work of the Continuation School was as follows "The school has been efficiently conducted, and given excellent preparation for pupils passing to the Techni- cal School ." At the same time the highest grant allowed was granted for all subjects taught in the School. The Science and Art Department which examines the draw- ing, classed the instruction as " Excellent," and also awarded the highest grant."

I.IIE EXASIINEE, SATURDAY JANUARY 19 1895.

THE OLD BARRACKS..

..... At the conclusion of the report the Chairman ex- pressed his appreciation of the public spirit of the Mayor in purchasing the old barracks which had been used for some years as a part of the school for technical train- ing. -(Applause.) Whatever opinion may have ex- isted as to the value of the purchase, he believed it was a valuable acquisition to the town, and if anyone had the idea that it could be purchased for less money, they had better disabuse themselves of that idea as soon as possible. They now felt more settled than they had for some time, and he hoped that they might soon set about making the building more suitable for its uses. He had great pleasure in asking the Mayor to distribute the prizes. His WORSHIP thanked the Technical Schools Corn- mittee for the honour they had done him in having asked him to distribute the prizes. In his younger days they did not hear much about technical schools. He then presented the prizes, with a few commendatory words to each recipient. LIST OF PRIZES.

Councilior SHAW-GREEN in proposing the best thanks of the meeting to the Mayor for his presence and distri- buting the prizes, said that the increase in the number of prizes fully justified the committee in taking over the School of Art. They had won four of the Scholarships given by the Lancashire County Council and they had been well helped by their chairman,who had at heart the welfare of the institute, and they had a very able secre- tary. The whole town, he thought, would be well satis- fied with what they had done, and the number of scholarships given showed that the committee were in earnest. _ _ .

Mr. C. S. BURGESS seconded the motion. Mr. MONKS endorsed all that had been said, and said it was always a matter of great pleasure to him to see the Mayor taking his full share of the intellectual work of the town as well as the municipal. They were also glad to see the Mayoress. The MAYOR suitably responded to the vote and in addition proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman for the eood work he had done in connection with the Technical School.—The hearty accordance of this last vote brought the proceedings to a fitting close. _

DARING BURGLARY AT SA.NKEY

BRIDGES.

BURGLARS IN A BEDROOM. A burglary of a daring nature was committed during the early hours of Monday, at Gatewharf Farm, Sankey Bridges, the house of Mr. Benjamin Grimshaw. There I were evidently two burglars at work, and their mode of operations showed that they were experienced hands. They obtained admittance by breaking a pane in the lower sash of the window of the back kitchen, and then pulled back the catch, and opened the top sash. The bottom sash had been secured by a patent catch, so that it was impossible to open that. After entering the kitchen the burglars proceeded to the pantry which they ransacked,and took a few coppers, a loaf, 61bs. of meat, and some sausages. They then evidently proceeded to the sitting-room of Mr. R. B. Matthews, the chairman of Little Sankey Parish Council, and made an examina- tion of every drawer and also Mr. Matthews' clothes, creating great confusion. They did not snowed in find- ing any money, but they took possession of a silver watch belonging to Mr. Matthews. Mr. Grimshaw's bedroom was also visited by one of the burglars, but Mrs. Grimshaw hearing the " click" which the door made on being opened 'looked round, and saw the man just about to rifle the clothes of Mr. Grimshaw, which contained a gold watch valued £3O and also £8 153. 3d. in cash. She screamed and woke her husband who at once gave chase, but the burglars had left the doors open to allow of their speedy exit,and on going through banged the doors after them, which prevented Mr. Grimshaw from catching them, and they thus escaped. Mr. Grimshaw and Mr. Matthews at once went for the police, who were soon on the spot. Upon examining the house, Mrs. Grimshaw's writing desk and a silver flask belonging to Mr. Matthews, valued £4. were found ready for taking away but the burglars ha not had time to take them. The dog was also found a- sleep in an outhouse,and it had evidently been drugged Later on in the day, a valuable silk muffler was also discovered to be missing, and it is probable that this was used to pack the missing provisions in. A few coppers were found during Monday by P.S. Devlin, and Mr. Edmunds, news agent, which appeared to have been lost by the burglars, and also a sherry bottle which had been broken owing to one of them falling over a barrow in his flight. The footsteps of the men were traced in the snow to the river, but here all traces were lost in the grass. The burglar who entered Mr. Grimshaw's bedroom was very distinctly seen by Mrs. Grimshaw owing to a paraffin stove being lighted at the time, and she says that she would be able to identify him again.

FOUR INQUESTS AT WARRINGTON.

BURNING FATALTIES AND FATAL FALLS.

Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, held inquests' at the Parade room en Wednesday morning, on the bodies of Annie Bibby, six years of age, 132, School Brow, and Thomas Jackson, four years, 97 Cartwright-st., War- rington. both of whom had died on the 12th inst., at the Infirmary, from burning injuries. In the first case Mrs.Bibby,wife of Joseph Bibby,plas- terer, 132, School Brow, commenced the evidence by stating that the deceased Annie Bibby, was her daugh- ter, and six years old. On Saturday the 12'h inst., de- ceased left her home about five minutes to 10 a.m. and went to witness's sister's house. Half-an-hour later witness received word that her daughter had got burnt. Witness went to her sister's house and on seeing her daughter, fell unconscious, and did not see the deceased again until at the Infirmary, when deceased, though conscious, could net explain how the accident; hap- pened.—Alice Backhouse, sister of last witness, and re- , siding at 24, Manchester-road, said on Saturday morn- ing, deceased came to her house at 10 a.m., and went upstairs with two of witness's children. The room they had gone to contained a large fire. Shortly after, ahe heard the children scream, and running upstairs she saw deceased in flames and told her little boy to wrap his coat round deceased. Returning far assistance, witness fell downstairs. She got up and told a man who was passing that a child was on fire up-stairs and he ran into the room and put the fire on deceased's clothes, out. Witness's daughter said that deceased had got a chip out of the fire and in doing so, her clothes caught fire.---James Threadgold, said that on Saturday morning he was passing last witness's house when he was called in, and rendered assistanee by wrapping a carpet round the deceased and extinguishedi the flames. The child was then taken to the Infirmary. I —Miss Isherwood, nurse at the Warrington Infirmary, ' deposed that deceased was admitted to the Infirmary, on Saturday morning about 11 o'clock, suffering from severe burns on the neck, arms and body. There was no hope of recovery from the first, and deceased died about four o'clock in the afternoon. A verdict was returned of" Accidental death." Martha Jackson, wife of Thomas Jackson, bricksetter, 97. Cartwright-street, Warrington, and mother of deceased, Th - --as Jackson, was the first witness in the second case. Deceased was four years old and on Saturday the 12th,inst.,she was upstairs dressing herself when deceased went down into the kitchen before her. A moment later she heard him scream and running down found him in flames. He was eonscious and said that he was leaning over the fire to get some cinders when his shirt caught fire. The next door neighbour came in and assisted in freeing deceased of his clothes. Deceased was taken to the Infirmary.--Elizabeth Hill, neighbour of last witness, corrobbrated, and said that she herself took the ,deceased to the Infirmary.—Miss Allen, nurse at the Infirmary, was present when deceased died there at a quarter to two in the afternoon. In this case also there was no chance of deceased's re- covery,and the burns were very severe about deceased's chest, arms, and face.—The same verdict of " Acci- dental death" was returned by the jury.

LYMM.

CURLING Marcu.—On Saturday a curling match was played on the private lake at Beechwood between High Legh and Beechwood curlers. At the c,al of time Beechwood had won by 22 shots. An excellent luncheon was provided for all the players on the ice by Mr. G. L. Dewhurst. .

NEWFIELD INSTETIITE.—The weekly entertainment ' was held in the Newfield Institute on Saturday night. Mr. T. P. Norris presided, and there was a good at- tendance. The entertainnvnt was of an excellent character, and songs, mandoline and guitar solos, Stc., were given by the following :—Messrs. Myrtle and Parker, Mrs. Ridgway, and the Misses Cookson, Lane, Grundy, Swanwick, and Morris. " EiscuzLoa's VIEW OF NOME LIFE."—On Monday night, the Rev. J. Wagstaffe, Northwich, gave a highly interesting and racy 'lecture to the members of the Wesleyan Mutual Improvement society on a " Bachelor's view of home life." The lecturer's graphic description of various homes and their inmates WAS marked by humour. pathos, and strong practical commonsense. and was keenly enjoyed by the audience. Mr. James Dodds presided. Hearty votes of thanks to lecturer and chairman concluded a thoroughly enjoyable evening. BEGGING AT Bnoom EDGE.—At the Lymm Police Court on Tuesday morning, before Mr. A. watkin, Michael Jordon, a tramp, was charged with begging at Broo m Edge on the previous afternoon. P.C. Sandbach proved the case, and Sergeant Garner stated the man had been begging at several houses in the district. He was a egular " bird of the road."—He was sent to gaol for even days. „

TOWN COUNCIL NOTES.

The Mayor opened the proceedings of the Town- Council on Tuesday evening last week,with the an- nouncement that he had received two corn munica tions—one from Mrs.Voisey acknowledging the vote ojcondolence passed at the previous meeting upon the death of her husband, and the other from the Warrington Property Owners Association. He did not state the nature of the communication from the singularly modest and retiring Property Owne Association, but suggested that the docume rs should be handed over—shall we say for inter- ment ?—to the General Purposes Committee, and this was assented to nem. con The minutes of the Watch Committee were.passed without incident, except that Councillor Beaman remarked upon the dangers of unlighted milk carts in the streets at night.

The Chairman r he Finance Committee, Coun- cillor Shaw Green, gave some interesting informa- tion relating t financial matters in moving the adoption of the committee's minutes. The commit- tee is about to take advantage of the " cheap " money that is now in the market by paying off the mnnicipal mortgages which are bearing a higher rate of interest than three per cent.' and replacing them by others on a lower scale of in- terest. The committee, it seems, has other pro- jets in hand for reforming the finance of the borough but—one thing at a time. Councillor Smethurst did not like the proposal to annually increase the salaries of the collectors, till a maximum sum was reached and he sarcastically argued that this was not the way pri- vate employers dealt with their servants, though the Mayor intormed him that it was the method adopted in most large concerns. The minutes of the Committee were approved.

ThelStreet Improvement and Lighting Commit tee are taking steps towards making new bye-laws for the regulatiOn of building and street-making, and Councillor Bennett took advantage of the opportunity to 'put in a plea for wider streets. He spoke a shade disre- spectfully of ,Warrington streets,characterising some of them as more like rabbit-warrens than streets. Councillor Bennett anticipates a glowing future for Warrington—when the Ship Canal gets thoroughly going and the docks are made. He thinks that Warringtonians are hardly living up to their pros- pects, for while they censure their ancestors for making narrow roadways. they commit the same blunder themselves. A street sixty feet wide is Councillor Bennett's ideal.

The Health Committee are also, on the recom- mendation of the Local Government Board, about to amend their bye-laws for the purpose of improv- ing the sanitation of the town,and the amendments I will apparently have special reference to milk dealers. In moving the adoption of this com- mittee's minutes Aid. Sutton imparted the'agree- , able information that Warrington is just now in an unusually heathy state. The death-rate has lately I been lower than ever before in the present century. The infant mortality had also been lower than at any time during th 3 last thirty or forty years, while on the other hand the birth-rate was high. On the subject of an inquiry into the question of a public abattoir, Alderman Sutton assured the Council that nothing would be done towards estab- lishing an abattoir without the sanction of the Council. Councillor Roberts thought the Corpora- tion, and particularly the Health and Sanitary Works Committee, were to be congratulated upon the results of many years labour as seen in the improved health of the town. In fact, he suggested • that Warrington might before long be figuring as a residential borough, a suggestion which caused some little amusement. Councillor Bennett urged that while the subject of revised bye-laws was under consideration the provision of open spaces in different parts of the borough should not be lost sight of. As for the abattoir, he believed ' the butchers were opposed to it, and their wishes should not be ignored.

In moving the minutes of the Sanitary Commit- tee, Councillor Monks commented upon the defec- tive manner in which some of the machinery at Longford had been constructed, and Councillor Heaton, when submitting the business of the Pav- ing and Sewerage Committee, claimed that that committee in looking after the paving of hack pas- sages and streets had had something to do with the improved health of the borough. The proposal of the Town Hall Committee to increase the grant to the bands from £2O to ..£5O a year seemed to meet with the approval of the Council, espe- cially after the Mayor had intimated that in the coming summer the bands would not confine their performances to the Park, but also play in the open spaces of the town. There was some talk upon the report of the Committee that the stables at. the Town Hall could not be con- verted into suitable offices, but no conclusions were come to.

The main business of the meeting was then reached, namely, the recommendation of the Markets Committee to purchase the old milita barracks near Bank Quay Station for the proposes of a cattle market. Alderman Black- h urst, on behalf of the Committee, set the ball roll- ing by enumerating the advantages of the purchase. A formidable opposition was soon developed, Coun- cillor Cannell leading the way. The Councillor was alarmed at the spread of municipal socialism in Warrington. The Corporation had been acquiring property all round the town and yet here was a pro- posal to buy another substantial chunk. Councillor Heaton and Alderman Francomb followed on the same side, the latter stating that he understood the barracks had been already purchased on behalf of the Corporation. The Mayor here interposed that he had purchased the property and if the Corpora- tion would not take it he was quite willing to keep it. Councillor Skelton quoted figures to show that the barracks as a cattle market could not possibly pay its way, while Councillor Plumpton hold that the purchase would be a very good bargain indeed. Alderman Hutchinson displayed unusual oratorical powers in opposing the scheme. He enlarged upon the general unsuitability of the barracks for the purposes it Was intended to put them to—upon the defective sewerage of that part of the town, the insanitary construction of the barracks cottages, and he displayed more than a parental solicitude for the welfare of scholars who attend People's College and Trinity Schools, whose health and limbs he declared would be jeopardised by the removal of the cattle fair. It was a long harangue and rather surprised the Council. It can hardly be said that the Alderman was fluent or particularly eloquent, but it must be admitted that he did dis- play a kind of dogged pertinacity and an invincible determination to get to the very end. It was now quite clear that there was a strong opposition to the making of the barracks into a cattle market, and Councillor Roberts suggested that the barracks should be bought as proposed but that the question of utilising it for a cattle market should be left over , for further consideration. This was agreed to by Alderman Blackhurst, and on a vote being taken the proposal was carried by fifteen votes to thir- teen. The Council has, therefore, decided to pur- chase the barracks, but has not yet sanctioned its use as a cattle market.

The rest of the business wag quickly disposed of. Councillor Roberts mentioned that the opinion of Mr. Pembroke Stephens. Q C., had strengthened the hands of the Rivers Committee in its dealing with the Ship Canal Company. In regard to the Fire Engine Committee, Alderman Hutchinson denied that it was necessary for a person who rang the fire bell to nigh tothe fire engine station and sign a document before the fire engine would turn out— at least so far as fires in the borough "were con- cerned. As far as our country cousins are concerned they have to sign 8. paper to make themselves liable for expenses before the brigade will go to their assistance.

DEATH OF MRS. BLOOMER.

Mrs. Amelia J. Bloumer, the advocate of thei Bloomer costnme. died on the last day of the old fear at Council Bluffs, lowa, at the age of seventy-seven. The costume which went by her name was first worn by Mrs. Bloomer in 1849. It resembled male attire.hei no' an onen-fronted jacket and loose trousers, the latter wide, like those of the Turk, but gathered in at the ankles. The Bloomer dress was adontecl by a few women in the West-end of London in 1851, but, though recommended by ROMP American ladies in popular lectures, it was soon totally discontinued.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT WARRINGTON.

The debate which was opened by members of the I.L.P. Warrington branch,at the Debating Society on Wednesday, last week, proved to be a most interesting one. The I.L.P. was present in full force, and speedily made their presence known by iloudly cheering their own side, and I am sorry to say, by hissing at the other side. This was a breach of "debating etiquette" which was naturally warmly censured. Mr. Gee, who introduced the subject, has evidently, got the whole subject at his finger ends, and he only appeared to be at a loss to keep his remarks within the bounds ot the prescribed time. Probably the best speech on the side of the I.L.P. was that of Mr. Ball, who should develop into a good speaker. Mr. Brouchemite was a shade too wild for serious consideration. When he accuses every rich man of being either a thief or the son of a thief—well, he gets "off-side," to use a footballer's expression. The I.L.P.'rs did not, my by means, have it all their own way, as effective speeches on the other side were made by Messrs. Horton, Gandy, Ains- worth and Hall.

Lovers of scenery and pretty views could not do better than take a walk through the country while the frost lasts, and some very pretty effects from frozen foliage will be observed. In all directions, huge trees, bushes, &c., will be seen drooping grace- fully under the accumulated weight of hoar-frost. Iron railings have increased almost ,twice in thick- ness, while the telegraph wires resemble " taut " ropes more than anything else. Though the days are dull, amateur photographers will do well to walk abroad and take a few choice snapshots of the glistening foliage, before the opportunity is gone.

The Board of Guardians were not long in decid- ing on Wednesday last week, who were to be their chairman, and vice- chairmen, for they elected the old officers in too. As another election of :officers willbe necessary in April, it was, indeed, scarcely worth while to make a change for that short time. Conse- quently the feeling that Messrs. W. Houghton, Davies and Pilling, should be re-elected, was per- fectly unanimous. Mrs. Clay received a hearty I welcome when she entered the Board room, where she was introduced to the members by Mr.Dignum. She made her " maiden" speech on the question of appointing a ladies' committee, and—this ques- tion, being the occasion of the first division or the Board—she was on the winning side.•

The recent elections have caused a great change in the personnel of the Board, and several of the new members give prothise of speedily coining to the front. The Rev. J. H. Riddette will be in no way a silent member, but Mr. Jacob Billinge who with Mr. Riddette, hails from Haydock. quite astonished the Board with his oratory. 11r. Bil- linge has a very denunciatory style of speaking. He , has a plentiful supply of adjectivest,and he accom- panies them with plenty of gesticulation, and gives all:the emphasis of a voice which would fill the new Public Hall with ease, let alone the small Board-room at the Workhouse. Mr. Billinge will make his mark on the Board of Guardians, and I am glad of this because he is on the progressive side.

The negotiations between the Warrington Cor- poration and the Manchester Ship Canal areinow in an advanced state, although for obvious reasons,the re ;ults are not given to the world at large. I hear that on Monday afternoon, the Ship Canal directors and the Rivers Committee will meet at the Town Hall to discuss the matter. It is to be hoped that the Rivers Committee will obtain some substantial concessions from the Company. Could they not pursuade the Company to commence the docks forthwith?

I am glad to hear that there is a prospect of work for a large number of people in one of the large works of the town. I understand that an order for cable has lately been received, which will probably last for two years. This is good news indeed, and it is to be hoped that it is only the first of good numbers of similar orders. Most of the large works in the town have not, I believe, been very busy for some time past, and a few large orders would be a very acceptable New Year's gift.

NIERLIN.

THE CHELFORD RAILWAY DISASTER

RESUMED INQUEST.

Mr. Yates, the coroner for East Cheshire, resumed on Friday his inquiry into the circumstances of the disaster which occurred on the L. and N.-W. Railway at Chelford, on December 22nd, by which 13 persons were killed and many injured. Major Marindin, the Board of Trade inspector, sat as assessor,the officials of the company watched the proceedings, and the relatives of deceased were legally represented. John Hyde, stationmaster at Chelford, said he accepted the responsibility for shunting operations. Au empt) waggon was thrown into the up line four-foot as tha express approached. It was a o ,ugh night. He was fully cognizant of the rule applying to shunting.—The Coroner having summed up, the jury returned a verdict of " Accidendal death," and recommended that the com- pany should arrange for shunting to cease when ex- presses were due.

PRECAUTIONS AGAINST THE FREEZ- ING OF WATER PIPES.

It is only moderately well-built houses warmed by ordinary hearth fires it is not easy to maintain a tem- derate of more than ten degrees Fahr. higher than that of the external air, and as more than ten degrees of frost have best' registered upon the screen there is fear of frost entering the dwelling. The following sugges- tions have been issued to prevent the disagreeable oc- currence of water pipes :—L During the frosty weather burn a gas jet in the cistern-room and bathroom in the night (and if the frost is very severe, during the day also), closing up the fireplace, door, window, and all crevices. 1. Draw a little water off occa- sionally from each of the taps, in order to see if the water runs. 3. So long as the hot water will run, keep a good fire in the kitchen and make it up last thing every evening so as to keep it in all night. 4. If the hot water will not run, leave all the taps open (tempor- arily). See that there is very little fire in the kitchen grate, and get a plumber to thaw the pipes ; this, if at- tended to, may prevent a bur. s.—lf the pipes get frozen up and cannot be thawed, there will be no danger in keeping a very small fire in the kitchen, taking care to place apiece ot iron at the back of the grate to block up the boiler hole ; this is better than putting the fire out, as the slight heat prevents the boiler from getting frozen up. 6. If the bath taps in either cistern should be frozen up, thaw them with boiling water, breaking the ice in the cistern, if any. 7. If a burst occur, be- fore sending for a plumber turn the stop-tap off, and open all the taps.

AN INTERESTING CHAPTER OF

WARRINGTON HISTORY.

All old Warringtonians will appreciate and he interested in the following report, which carries us back a period if 50 years, and the subject dealt with below wi no doubt be remembered by many. On Sunday evening last week the members of the Golborne-st.Baptist Chapel were convened to attend a special church meeting,the object of which was to receive at the bands of Mr. B. Few, deacon, and Mr. Fernley, secretary, a magnificent service of com- munion plate, which they, on behalf of the church, had received from Mrs. Morris, Southport, niece of the late Mr. J. B. Edelsten, and from whose exe- cutors the church at Golborne-st. purchased their property some 20 years ago, and in wboseposses- sion the plate had been. The service, which con- sists of five pieces, and is a valuable and handsOme- one, has been, by the kindness of Mrs. Morris, put into thorough repair and renovated as when new. Its chief value .is in its associations, for it is the same service which was used by The church wor- shipping in the same building in the earlier part of its history, having been bought and presented by the late Mrs. Edelsten to the church meeting there in 1852, under the ministry of the Rev. Mr. Hart- land. The building, which was built in the year 1811. has had a somewhat chequered career,and has been in turn the meeting places of several different denominations,passed, about the year 1851,into the hands of the late Mr. J. B. Edelsten, from.whose executors it was purchased by the church now occupying it in the year 1876. At the time the Baptists took possession of the building Mrs. 'Weis- ten. who has recently passed away, expressed a desire that at some future time the service migilt again be brought into use in the old "Salem" chapel, and in accordance with her desire, after a lapse of at least 40 years, the communion plate was . Again brought into use on Sunday evening last. Naturally, the plate was • an object of _admiration, id:if:Utmost, Len-light be said, of veneration.

c.fiThiY.-EFFECTS OF

ELECTRICITY

A curious case has just been recorded in which an electric current was found to be generated. by a plate of anificial teeth. A patient consulted his doctor on account of u severe pain in his tongue. But the sufferer was assured that there was nothing the matter, lie then paid a visit tojiis dentist, who informed him that his teeth s‘ere perfectly , sound. Being, however, dissatisfied, he called upon an electrician whom he knew, and asked if it were possible that he could have any electricity in his mouth. On examining the teeth his friend found that two metals were used to fix them to a corn-. position plate. To theae metals wires were then attached and connected to a galvanometer. Then the teeth were replaced in the patient's mouth and the metals moistened with saliva. No sooner was- this done than the galvatfometer showed quite a large current from so small a source—enough, lt is stated, to cause ulceration and severe pain when long continued upon so sensitive an organ as the tongue. The plate was Covered with an insulating varnish, and thenceforward all the trouble ceased.. .

IF we may judge from some of the Mr. John Burns 'accounts, Mr. JOHN BURNS, the in America. very Radical member of Parlia- ment for Battersea, has had a flattering re- ception on the other aide of the Atlantic, from the workmen at all events. He has done five weeks lecturing and travelling for the Labour Federation of America, and his experiences have been varied. At one mining community in the Rocky Mountains, some nine thousand feet above sea level, a children's snow tournament was organised in his honour, but on the other hand the selected chairman of one of his meetings at Chicago hesitated to preside, because he was afraid "there might be some shooting." Mr. BURNS had been speaking out strongly against municipal corruption,and language of this kind did not suit the " boodlers " of Chicago, who are, it appears, quite unscrupulous enough to resort to " shooting " or any other violent methods to accomplish their ends. However, the shooting did not come off, and the member for Battersea I was allowed to leave Chicago with a whole I skin, even after describing it as " a pocket edi- tion of hell." Mr. BURNS naturally, considering his antecedents and his mission, looked at the United States mainly from a workman's point of view, and he did not find them an industrial Paradise, in spite of the Protection which has been vaunted so much. There are slums in some of the large towns which are worse than any that can be seen in England, and the Italian quarter in Chicago is a "horror unspeakable," the streets and alleys reeking with garbage and Elth,which lie in heaps from November to April. The locomotive engineers are the princes of American labour) and they can earn from £1 to £7 a week. But the American artisan has to work harder and longer hours than the English one, and he has fewer holidays. With regard to bricklayers, Mr. BURNS says that at New York and Chicago he found them "laying nearly twice as many bricks as a London workman. Indeed, as one foreman said to me, they work too hard. They may get two shillings an hour, but that does not as a rule last more than six or seven months—a whole year's labour is crammed into that period. The pressure is tremendous." Mr. BURNS adds, "skilled men, like compositors, who are very well paid,are begin- ning to be affected by machinery, and one of the immediate problems of that industry-is for the men to adapt themselves to the new state of things. Then you should remember that house rent is twenty to fifty per cent, higher in the States than here, and that company rents—that is to say, rents paid to the coporation which owns the men's labour—and ,the truck system eats deep into the wage fund." The conditions of unskilled labour are evidently worse in America than in England. The labourers have to compete for em- ployment with Italians, Hungarians, Bohemians, and even Chinese, the standard of living of whom is far beneath that of the Englishman or native; born American. Miners are in the same category as unskilled labourers, and Mr. BURNS found foreigners engaged in exhausting labour in the coke region for three shillings a day. "The mor- tality amongst this class is horrifying, and throws a ghastly light on municipal corruption. The death-rate in one block of tenement buildings holding 5,000 people in New York in 1893 was 346 per annum, that is to say, 63 per 1,000." The member for Battersea is decidedly of opinion that men's lives are used up faster in the American industrial system than in our own. The street cars and railways slaughter the people at some- thing like a wholesale rate, and he makes the as- tounding statement that at the level crossings of Chicago railways, as many people are slain as are in England killed all over our railways including passengers and workers. There are, however, two institutions which the Americans lavish expendi- ture upon—the prisons and the schools. The Christmas dinner at Columbia Penitentiary con- sisted of roast turkey,mince pie, beef, oranges an -I apples, bread and potatoes. The prisoners elect their own governing officers; they live and sleep largely in common and they play cards or chess or read newspapers at their will. On the subject of the schools Mr. BURNS grew enthusiastic. The American grudges nothing for his schools. There is no starving or stinting of education. The teachers are well paid, hold a good social position, and have a large moral influence. In fact, in no country in the world is education so well cared for—surely a hopeful feature in the social life of the Great Republic.

S

THE subject of "unearned Mere- Unearned Increments. ment " was dealt with in an inter- esting fashion at the beginning of the week by Mr. HALDANE, Q.C., M.P. Mr. HAL- DANE referred in his speech more particu- larly to the case of London, but the ° subject itself is not one of local imper-' tance only. The unearned increment on the„ value of land is reaped by landowners in every " centre of population and industry. London, of course, offers the most startling object lesson because it has a larger population than is con- 4.4 tamed in any other city on the face of the earth. , The facts and figures adduced by Mr. HALDANH were de3ply impressive in their way. The land upon which London stands would, he said, if let at its agricultural value barely be worth £16,000 a year. Bat at the present time the rent paid for that land, apart altogether from the houses and buildings which have been Arected upon it,amounts to no less a sum than £16,000,000 How has this annual value been multiplied thousandfold? It has not certainly been added by the landlords although they are enjoying the benefits of it. Nor can it be said, that the value has been given by any one section of the population. It was due, as Mr. Haldane pointed ont,to the simple growth of the population The people of London as a whole are the creators% of all these millions of annual value which are diverted into the private pockets of the ground: landlords. And the increase of value is going - on at a rapid rate. Every year that passes sees another 300,000; added be' the rent-rolls of the owners of the land. Mr. HALDANE might well contend that it is high time that this enriching of a comparatively few, privileged individuals at the expense of the whole community should come to an end. He does not, propose that the unearned increments of the past should be taken from the ground landlords, but het. doessuggest that future increases should be turned,4 into the public coffers. A similar proposal watt. made many years ago by JOHN Sruaar MILL, and had it been adopted at that time all the progres- sive English towns and cities would now have been in a happy position. Probably, in many cases they would have been drawing from ground values alone a largerincome than they are now receiving from the whole of their rates. The rates of, London amount, for instance, to between' seven and eight millions a year, and this amount which would be completely covered by twenty. years growth of the metropolitan land value. Other cities and towns in which population is increasing would be similarly benefited by the reform advo- ' cated by Mr. HALDANE. With every increase in population there is an increase in ground values, and as it is the population which creates these additional values there can be no argument, ne• the ground of equity, against the appropriation of the "unearned increments" of the future for the common benefit.

THE Vicen of Runcorn, CA-sroX The Educa- tion Controversy WOOD, like many other clergy- m3n of the Church of England,has a weakness for the role of critic of School BoardS:, and School Boardeducation. But, af tor all, his-- AI indictment is not a very formidable one. In fact,. , it all depends upon the point of view one takes,. whether his remarks at a congregational tea meeting on Monday. may not be taken as an eulogy of School:Boards or as a condemnation of of voluntary schools. He practically admitted that the education given at Board schools is - hotter than that given at voluntary schools, but he [claims that the voluntary education is the cheaper. Well, probably there are few advocates of School Boards who will care to argue that point. A good thing is worth paying for, and education is certainly no exception to the rule. Whether we look at the matter from an individual 1 or a national point of view the starving of the schools in which the great bulk of the children of the country receive all their education is the very unwisest of economies. If the standard of educa- ' tion in the voluntary schools in Runcorn, orlanr where else, falls distinctly below that of the, Board schools, there is no better evidence needed - that voluntaryism is a drag on the educational machine for which there is not much to be said. , Perhaps this explains the " very small response" to the appeals for support of the free schools a which Canon WOOD complains.

We are pleased to state that Mr. F. J. Norman, J.P., of Lyndhurst, Higher Runcorn, who has been for some days suffering from an imflamed throat, is now recovering from what might have been a most serious illeess. PRIMITIVE METHODIST CHAPEL, RUNCORN. The annual meeting of the trustees of this chapel was held on'Tuesday evening, when it was resolved to take off the roof, reslate it, and further to gener- ally repair and clean the building. The total cost is estimated at about £l5O. THE SHIPPING TRADE AT RUNCORN.

During the week,the shipping trade at Runcorn has showed great activity. There has been a.mark- ed increase in the number of vesse:s arriving at Runcorn docks, and many others have been char- tered for Runcorn, and expected to arrive within the next few days. It is to be hoped that this may be the beginning of brisker times at Runcorn docks. —No further tidings have been heard of the Betsy Nichols, in charge of Captain Roberts, of Runcorn, or the Louisa, in charge of his son, Captain F. Roberts.

RUNCORN FRRRY.

The necessity for the early re-opening of Runcorn Ferry was under discussion at the meeting of the Parliamentary and General Purposes Committee of the Urban Council on Thursday. The newly- appointed chairman of the committee (Mr.P.Speak- man, J.P., C.C.) presided. It was considered that pressure should be brought to bear on the London and North-Western Railway Company, and the Manchester Ship Canal Company, to have this desired end brought about at the earliest possible date. A committee consisting of Messrs. Speak- man, Imison, and Lightburn was appointed to con- fer with the Widnes Corporation, if deemed neces- sary, on the matter. THE NEW TRADES HALL AT RUNCORN.

On Thursday a meeting of the Parliamentary and General Purposes Committee of the Runcorn Urban Council was held, when the draft of the Trust deed of the above-mentioned hall, was under considera- tion. A digest of the deed was given by the Law Clerk (Mr. F. A. Lake), who said it would be advis- able that a committee should be appointed to confer with Mr. J. T. Brunner, M.P., and the repre- sentatives and the trades societies. The following were appointed, a committee: Mr. P. Speakman (chairman of the committee), Mr. E. Imison (chair- man of the council), Mr. Lightburn, Mr. Williams and Mr. James Kirkham. • Sr. JOHN'S PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH, RUNCORN. A service of song entitled " Nan," was rendered on Wednesday evening in the above-named church, under the auspices of the Band of Hope. There was a very full choir, the members of which entered heartily into the parts assigned to them. Mr. Mat- thew Thomas was the conductor, and Mr. Pritchard presided at the harmonium. There was a large attendance, and the performance was greatly ap- preciated, the audience being deeply impressed by the striking moral lessons conveyed by the service of song. The pastor of the church, the Rev. J. Pal- ford Williams, gave the conne•Jtive readings. The workers of St. John's Band of Hope deserve every.l encouragement in the succesOul work they are do- I ' ing in this department of Christian activity. TEACHERS' SOIRE E AT GB ERNWAY-R0 AD BOARD

Saloom. On Friday evening, the teachers in connection with the Runcorn Board Schools spent a social evening together. Music and games were indulged in, and suitable refreshments were provided. A most enjoyable evening was spent. HALF-Ilona TALSi IN THE LIBRARY.

RUNCORN WOMEN'S TEMPERANCE SOCIETY. - -

The Runcorn Branch of the Women's Temperance Society held their annual meeting on Monday even- ing, in Brunswick school. The proceedings com- menced with a tea of which 35 partook, the tables being presided over by Mrs. H. Morris and Mrs. T. Bowyer. At the subsequent business meeting Mrs. R. Morris occupied the chair. Miss Littler, the secretary, read the report, which showed that dur- ing the past year the society had held three public meetings, while Mrs. _Metcalfe, of Burnley, had successfully carried on a week's mission under the auspices of the society. Forty pledges had been taken during the year, and it was believed that much good had been accomplished. The treasurer's report, submitted by Mrs. Simkin, showed a balance of £4 after meetings all the expenses, which had been heavier than usual. Both reports were adopted, and the electi3n of officers was proceeded with. Mrs. It. Morris was elected president, and this lady acknowledged the honour in a graceful and appropriate speech. The other appointments were, treasurer, Mrs. T. Cooke ; secretaries, Mrs. R. Harrop and Mrs. John Temper- Icy; committee, Mesdames Bisbrown, Bramhall, Brackenbury. J. D. Jones, Leathwood, G. Marshall, Leach, A. Millinizer, Posnett, Reynolds, Richardson, Simkin, Wilton,Heatherington,and Stafford; Misses Dobson, Littler, and Norman. Arrangements were made for holding the annual public meeting in February.

TITF, DISTRESS AT RUNCORN.

Active efforts are being made to cope with the distress in the town. In addition to the tickets for food distributed by the local committee, who it may be mentioned gave orders to the value of over £B9 last week, several other agencies have been commenced. Owing to distress among boat- people, caused by the frost, a soup kitchen was opened at the Seamen's Mission on Friday, at noon. and was continued until Tuesday. About 600 people were supplied with soup of excellent qua- lity. The promoters of the scheme were Mr. R. Abel, Mr. Crosby (top locks), and Mr. Jonathan Iforsfleld, who was assisted by several friends The work was organised by Mr. Shaw, missionary. On Saturday, through the generosity of Mr. S. Yar- wood and Mr. J. Jones, of the North Western lintel, 60 people above 70 years of age were the recipients of a parcel of tea and sugar. Of those who re- ceived the gifts one had attained the age of 91 years, eleven were over 80, and the rest have lived above the allotted span of life. The total ages came to over 4,520. On Monday morning, and each morning during the past week, over 100 school children at the National Schools, Church-st., have been, through the kindness of several gentlemen, provided daily with free breakfasts, consisting of cocoa and bread and marmalade,and treacle. Several , of the children have, in addition, received boots and clothing. The Bev. Canon wood, assisted by the head teachers and otners, have carried out the work. Mr. A. li. Norman has provided over 160 persons with col.—The weekly meeting of the Relief Committee was held on Thursday evening at the Town Hall, when tickets for relief to the amount of about £9O were distributed. The follow- ing additional subscriptions were reported :—Lord Ellesmere and the Bridgewater Trustees, £lO ; Mr. G, W. II ayeA, £lO ; Mr. W. Hayes jun., £lO ; Mr. G. Lewis, £2 25.; Mr. G. W. Weston, .£1 is.; Mr. W. Lloyd Davies, £1 ls.; Mr. WE. Leigh and workmen, its. The United Alkali Campany have decided to increase their donation of £5 to £lO for the next four weeks. !gr. Morrison writing on behalf of the Comp4ny stated that there was no immediate pro- bability of the works getting into full swing again,, adding that the workmen 571.10u1d be recommended to seek employment elsewhere. At Weston, the Rev. W. N. Stables is daily providing dinner for the poor children of the village. The building, which is to serve as a reading room, is being utilized for the purpose, and nearly 100 children have been accommodated each day. 31r. T. T. Sill,the manager of the chemical works, has also a soup kitchen in operation for tile unemploy ed.

THE EX4NIINEIt SATURDAY, JANUARY 19, 1895. WARRINGTON.

A STREET INCIDENT.

. '•;i

On Saturday in the neighbourhood of Schools brow, a horse and trap, belonging to Mr. Chantry, confectioner, Sankey-st. and Ilorsemarket-st., had been temporarily left by the driver in Battersby- lane, when the animal was frightened by some children. It at once began to gallop, and turning suddenly into Crossley-sr., knocked off the wheels of the conveyance against the wall. An old woman who happened to he near was knocked down, it is supposed by the re-bounding of one of the wheels from the wall. Broken as the trap was, the horse dragged it through Cockhedge and School-brow, until it was ultimately stopped by some workmen near a stonemason's yard. The woman referred to was subsequently attended by Dr. Peacocke, but was found to have sustained very little injury. LAncEsi TAILOR-MADE JACKETS. in Brown. Tan, and Black Fit and Fsshion guiranteed.--BvAsia., 17, Sankey-street, Warrington.—Advt.

BIITTERII4,REET-ST. MISSION HILL. An excellent entertainment was given in tt e Mission Hall, Buttermarket-st., on Saturday night, in aid of the funds of the mission,by Mr. W. Whit- taker, assisted by other friends. The entertainment consisted of songs, solos, and selections on the con- certina (with farm yard and various other imita- tions), and was much appreciated. WARRINGTON BRANCH SOCIAL DEMOCRATIC FEDERATION.

The first meeting of the above branch was held on Monday, at the S.D.F. Hall, Friars'-gate, when the election of officers took place. The President- elect, Comrade IVilliam Cosgroves, in his presiden- tial address, said they intended to conduct a most vigorous progaganda this summer. He contended that Social Democracy was the only factor that would emancipate the wage-earner from the wage- slavery of to-day. The political parties, whose principles are economically played out, had, he con- tended, never lifted a finger in the. interest of labour, but both parties had done all they possibly could to stem the tide of historical progress and social development. The following were appointed as the executive:—President, Comrade William Cosgroves ; secretary, Comrade A. Brouchemite ; treasurer, Comrade William Codling ; committee : Comrades Wm. Gee, Arthur Chaplin, Wm. Lester, Charles Marlow, Wm. Drewett,_and John Ratcliff.
