THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT

RUNCORN.

BY OUR RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT.

I was pleased to notice the alacrity shown by our local officials in having the snow, which fell so heavily throughout Saturday night, removed from the streets. Quite a large number of "unemployed " alen were engaged on Sunday morning to do the work, and they quickly made a passage through the snow, to the great convenience of pedestrian. Thi was in striking contrast to the rather dilatory manner of dealing with it during the previous week, which I commented upon in the last issue of this paper. In justice to those responsible for the. work, their prompt attention to the matter on this last occasion now deserves noting. The men were again engaged on the work on Monday, but if the statement which I hear, be true, that the men are paid at the handsome rate of 2s. per day for re- moving the snow, lam rather inclin. d to think that advantage is being taken of the men's poverty.

Praiseworthy efforts to mitigate the distress which unfortunately is so prevalent in the town, are being made. In addition to the distribution of relief tickets for food,an endeavour is being made to provide tree breakfasts at the Parish schools for the school children. On Monday, over 100 children were provided with a substantial break- fast, and this has been repeated each morning dur- ing the week. Shoes have also been provided for some of the children. This has proved au effective way of getting the children to school,some of whom would most likely have otherwise stayed away. A soup kitchen was also opened for the benefit of boat-people and their children on Friday last at the Seamen's Institute, and was continued until Tues- day. Through the generosity of Mr. S. Yarwood and Mr. J. Jones, of the North-Western Hotel, sixty people above 70 nears of age were on Saturday pre- sented with a parcel of tea and sugar. This reminds me that the Old People's Treat has not been pro- vided this year as usual, but surely the committee who have previously arranged the treat will not allow it to fall through, as the need of it is perhaps more generally felt at the present time than ever before.

The Rev. Canon Maitland Wood at the Runcorn Parish congregational tea meeting on Monday, in referring to the distress in the town, stated that large fortunes had been made in Runcorn, and that some of the people who had made them were now living away from the town. Ile thought if they were made aware of the distress, they would, had they any regard for the place where they had made their money, contribute towards the distress fund. Perhaps some of them will take the hint and send a donatian for the assistance of those who have as- sisted then to make their fortunes, but who may now be on the verge of starvation.

In the same address alluded to, our Rev. Canon took opportunity to preach the blessedness of voluntary schools. If his hearers were in any way dissatisfied wit) the quality of the address which be delivered for their delectation, they were fully compensated in quantity for he "held forth" for at least forty minutes. His chief argument for the continuance of voluntary schools was that of the saving of the rates. But the question arises are not the Board schools with the additional expendi- ture ? The Vicar admits that the teachers in Board schools are Letter paid than those in voluntary schools, and that therefore the school Board are able to command the best teachers. There is not the slightest doubt that the improved condition in the lot of day school teachers is directly attribut- able to Board schools, and further, that the chil- dren attending Board schools in many places, re- ceive a better education than was give' in middle class schools some time ago, and perhaps under far healthier conditions. Few will deny that the Church of England has done much in educatlng the masses, but denominational education is almost spent out, and is now struggling for life. There are even members of the Church of England who would pre- fer oeculos to sacerdotal education in schools, and this latter element is ti• evidtnce in some of the voluntary schools of to-day.

The Ven. Archdeacon Blakeney, who gained such a hold on the working classes in Sheffeld, and!who was buried on Tuesday at Ecclesall, Sheffield, was a brother-in-law of Mrs. Preston, wife of the Rev. Dr. Preston, vicar of of Holy Trinity Church, Runcorn. He was also one of the trustees of Holy Trinity Church. Prior to going to Sheffield in 1877, the deceased gentleman,was vicar of St. Paul's church. Claughton, Cheshire. tie had attained the age of 70 years.

WESTON.

A GOLDEN-WEDDING PRESENT.—At the meeting of the Weston Bible Mutual Improvement Class on Mon- day evening, Mr. Somerville ( secretary), in moving a resolution congratulating the president of the class, Mr. John Ford, and Mrs. Ford, on the attainment of their golden wedding on the 31st ult., said the members could not allow such an event to pass without placing on record how much they valued and appreciated the services of Mr. Ford in connection with the class. The success of the class was attributable to a great extent to the interest he took in it, and the valuable advice and assistance •he so willingly rendered. Mr. Basle- burst seconded the proposition, and in doing so said he was very pleased indeed to note that Mr. Ford had at- tained so interesting an event in him life, and hoped that he and Mrs. Ford would yet be spared for many years tolconae. The resolution was supported by Messrs. Kirkham, Dodd and Spruce, and enthusiastically carried. The secretary then called upon Miss Edith Smith as the youngest member of the class to present Mr. Ford with the two following books : Thompson's " Land and the Book " and Canon Farrar's" Life of St. Paul," which were ziven in remembrance of the occa- sion. Miss Smith, in making the prstentation, asked the acceptance of the books as a token of their affection, regard, and esteem. In reply, Mr. Ford said that he was very glad indeed to hear that his efforts on behalf of the class were so much appreciated by the members.

The present applicants were Messrs. C. Scott, E. Sutton, A. Wilson' W. Garner H. Clays, T. Rogers, E. O. Wal- ker, and B. Antwiss. It was decided that the person engaged should have the qualification of a plumber. EARLESTOWN WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL PRIZE DISTRIBUTION.

The annual distribution of prizes to the scholars attending the Wesleyan Sunday School, embraced in the round 0 class, which includes those who have been late or absent on any single occasion during the year, took place on Sunday afternoon in the chapel. Toe Rev. J. Bartley presided,and he was accompanied in the rostrum by Messrs. Cohen of Park-road, F. Clare, W. Pearson, G.. Clarke, W. Clare, and P. Parr secretary.—Besides the children there was a largo attendance of parents and others interested. After formally opening the meeting with singing and prayer, Mr. Parr, the secretary, read the annual school report, as follows " In taking a retrospect of the year 1894, we do not feel that sense of satisfaction at the result ot our work as it has been our wont in former years, due no doubt in a very large degree to the unsettled state we were in during ;July. August and September, owing to the alterations to the school premises ; in fact we are not yet quite settled down, and it is questionable whether we ever shall be until we have premises of our own. Yet, speaking generally, we have much c,1113P for thankfulness and even rejoicing. Our Heavenly Father has been very kind and merciful to us, not one of our workers have been taken away by death, and we are to day, excepting one or two who have resigned, the same band of workers as last year. We are also thankful to our friends and supporters for their kindly interest and help during the past Tear. We deeply appreciate their thoughtful- ness, and rely with implicit confidence upon the like for the year 1895. Our school is divided into 3 sections, viz., senior, junior, and infants, and again subdivided into 32 classes. Teachers and officers on the books 74, of which 39 are required each Sunday. Scholars on the books 475, last year 415, increase 60. Average attend- ance, morning : Teachers 68 per cent., scholars 32 per cent. Afternoon : Teachers 85 per cent., scholars 60 per cent. Teachers, members of society 40; scholars, including those in junior classes 84. With reference to the attendance, he was sorry it was not as good as in former years. They would notice the difference between the afternoon and morning attendance: The attend- ance in the morning was causing them very serious thought, especially amongst the major portion of tho school. There seemed to be an idea that the school was not for them in the morning, but he wculd ask them to disabuse their minds of this, and turn up as well in the morning as they did in the afternoon. The Juvenile Missionary Society has this year far exceeded cur highest expectations, due very largely to the untirine energy of its secretary, Mrs. G. Warburton. The 70 , collectors have gathered together in pence and half- pence the noble sum of over £55 towards our Home and Foreign Missions, and we shall now stand second in the circuit. The Band of Hope and Temperance Society have been very active, holding fortnightly and'monthly meetings, with an average attendance of 182. These meetings are very attractive, mad we promise on behalf of the committee that even still more attrac- tive and instructive programmes will be arranged if the friends will attend, and we would especially urge our young people to make an effort. The Round 0 Cia,93 has not been taken up by the scholars so eagerly as in former years. Perhaps it is owing to our being a little more strict in observing the details of this system. Yet the books on the table though not quite so numer- ous are in quality equal, if not superior, to those of former years, and, taken as a whole, speak well as regards the class of readinr; of our scholars, each recipi- ent having chosen his or her own prize. In addition to the Round 0 prizes we have this year to present three others to the best reciters in a competition which took place the first Sunday in September last. Seven scholars took part viz. : 1 out of the senior school, and 6 from the junior. The successful competitors were, senior school, Frank Emerton, junior school, let Janet Picket, End John W.Gilmour. The financial statement showed, receipts, including a balance of £l7. is. 6d. from last years £79 us. 9d. and expendi- ture £65 is. 51, leaving a balance of £l4 10. 4d. During the year a few of our scholars have been taken from us by death, and we would take this opportunity of tendering to the parents and friensls thus bereaved, our deepest sympathy and assurance of our prayers." Mr. Cohen of Park-road then gave an interesting address, directed principally to the teachers. - . . .

. . The Rev. J. Bartley in making the distribution of nearly 100 volumes, said he felt it a very great honour to have been asked to distribute the prizes. He knew most of the books to be given, and he could say that they ought to be gratified at the selection. There was nothing shoddy. . . .

During the afternoon a couple of sacred solos were sung by Miss Lily Barr, and Miss Ethel Collinge.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

(BY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIBUTOR).

I should think the most inveterate grumblers have during the latter end of last week, and the beginning of this week, had quite sufficient variety in regard to the atmospherical conditions to satisfy their peculiar tastes. Frost, snow, and rain we have had in plenty, and just as enthusiastic skaters were beginning to enjoy the pleasure of the ex- hilarating exercise afforded by a good sheet of ice, the demon thaw suddenly put in an appearance, and dispelled the anticipations of a Rood time. Newton lake was frozen over, and on Saturday a arge number of people, despite the strong wind which prevailed, disported themselves upon its urface, which was in splendid condition. On Sunday, however, the thaw set in and by Monday the ice was covered with water. In spite of this, a skating match was held on the ice, but the crowds of people who were spectators, had the good sense to rigidly confine themselves to the banks, leaving the ice free to the two contestants. Had the people been foolhardy enough to venture on the ice, I am, afraid we abould have had a case or two for th coroner.

Not to be outdone by its neighbour at Newt on ' Earlestown has instituted a cycling club, but,mind you, it is to be of a highly respectable character. Its rules which have been drawn up, are quite a study, and the most particular could ind nothing nom to cavil at. The memterehip of the club in not to exceed 20,and so far as the enrollment has iaroceeded, the intentions of the originators have peen upheld. Of course, this is not the first effort bthat has been made to get up such a club, but judging from the standing of the supporters in this case, the present effort should be a i.uccessful one. I have only one objection to it, and that is its evidently expensive character. I am net gifted with a great share of this world's goods, and what little I have, must be carefully guarded. However enthusiastic I may be as a cyclist, I am not pre- pared to go the length mapped out by the officials of the club. Occasionally, a run would come as a pleasant relaxation, but I am afraid there are but few cyclists in Earlestown who will care to indulge in the weekly social rune, on the scale suggested at present.

The introduction of working-men, OR the District Council, does not seem to be working as smoothly at If aydock, as one would wish, and I fancy the chairman, who was chairman of the late happy Board, ends his position not ex- ' actly an enviable one. At the first meeting he was given to understand that there was a good deal of courtesy in re-electing him as chairman, but that there was to be no monopoly in the business. Of course that was superfluous, because the election of chairman rests with the members of the Council. At the second meeting, which was a special one, a contentious member, enjoyed himself by making the chairman as uncomfortable as he possibly could, delighting in extending his remarks, when asked by the chairman to be brief. In fact, he point- blank refused to come to the end of his harangue, although the chairman intimated that be liad another important *engagement. It was not until he threatened to vacate the chair, that the windy councillor condescended to come :to a conclusion. This is how they do thing. at Elnydock, and judg- ing from the composition of the Council, we expect some lively doings in that quarter.

Whatever are the ratepayers of Earlestown think- ing about? They must be asleep,or they don't care two straws what becomes of the tows.ship. They hive allowed a majority of Councillors to be electsid who are favourable to one end of the township,snd who,if one may believe all that he hears, will before long, land us in a position which will cause U 8 to dig deep into our pockets. At Newton they are managing things rather differently. There is a healthy Ratepayers' Committee in existence, and they are in possession of a number of wires, which they are pulling with a vengeance. They meet in secret cooclave, and hatch plans. tor the welfare (?i of the township. The result of these gatherings will be seen at the meetings of the Council, but if the ratepayers ol Earlestown wish to check any high-handed measures, they must be up and doing else they will without a doubt, ere long find them selves subservient to the handful of ratepayers at Newton, and this after years of upward toil.

A WARRINGTON BAILIFF Our WITTED. On Monday afternoon a Warrington debtor named Nevins, who had been committed to Walton Gaol. for thirty-six 'days, effected his escape from his bailiff custodian in a simple but efficacious manner. Ile was entrusted to a new bailiff,in whose company he travelled quietly by train towards his destina- tion. Shortly after leaving the train the prisoner halted in front of a public-house door, and, with a look of relief on his face, said to the bailiff, " Oh, just wait outside here, and I'll go in ana fetch out the money in a minute." The bailiff, taken off his guard, let his min go, and waited patiently about until he would appear. Instead of getting the money out, Nevins got himself out as speedily as possible, but by a different door, , whereby he made good his escape. Needless to say the bailiff was very much mortified upon finding that he bad been so well deceived. TIM PROPOSED LADIES' VISITING COMMITTEE AT

LOCAL QUArtTER SESSIONS CASES:

At the Liverpo-ol Quarter Sessions which were opened at Liverpool on Tuesday. Alexander Brad- ley (27), a labourer, pleaded guilty to having, at Warrington, on December 14th, feloniously stolen two boot., the property of Samuel Hamer. He was sent to goal for one month. William George Jen- kins (31), joiner, was found not guilty with having at Newton, on December 10th, stolen a silver Geneva watch, a metal case, and four half-crowns, the property of John Bridge.

. . PAINFUL SCENE IN CHURCII-STREET.

A painful and exciting incident occurred in Church-street on Tuesday afternoon, during the progress of the funeral of a baby from a residence in that thoroughfare. A party of mourners had just seated themselves inside the coach when the horse attached to it took fright. It ran at great speed up Manchester-road in the direction of the Cemetery, and all efforts to stop it proved fruitless until it had reached Robson-street where it ran into the parapet,aud the vehicle upset,and the mourners and coffin thrown were out. Beyond a temporary shock however, they escaped uninjured, and the party afterwards walked to the Cemetery.

RECITAL_ AT BOLL-STRIIET WESLEYAN CHURCH.

Miss Florence Bourne gave a grand recital at Bold-street Wesleyan schoolroom on Monday even- ing, when there was a good attendance. The pro- gramme. was interspersed with musical selections by the Masters Agate of Manchester, whose exposi- tions on the piano and violin were of a very high order, and were warmly appreciated. Miss Bourne's programme was of a very varied character, and she kept up the interest of her auditory from beginning to end. Her first recital was " The forsaken Merman," by Matthew Arnold which she gave in a very appropriate manner. "The chariot race," from "Ben Hur," was next riven in a very realistic style and this was followed by "The dancing ghost," by, Egan Mew, which was written expressly for Miss iFlorence Bourne. Her selections from Shakespeare's ' Merchant of Venice," were given with great his- trionic skill, and the next piece, " Tom's little star," created much amusement, both by the matter and the manner of the recital. A selection from Tenny- son's popular piece," Guinevere," followed, and an American piece, " Matinal reflections," tickled the risibilities of the audience. The programme was brought to a close by " The Orphant Annie," by Riley, in which Miss Bourne imitated the voice of an old woman with great success. Miss Bourne possesses a voice of great flexibility and has all the power of an actor, so that she was able to present every piece to the audience, to the greatest advan- tage. The musical pieces given by the Bros. Agate included violin solo, airs from " Faust," violin 8010, "Carnival of Venice," pianoforte solo, "Alice, where Art Thou ?"

ODDFELLOWS' SUPPER AT RUNCORN

The annual supper in connection with the Loya• Wright's Pride Past Grands' Lodge. of Runcorn, was held on Thursday evening at the New Inn. There was a represents tiv. sttendance. The post financial pro- ceedings rosou iid ed over by Past Grand Joseph Pritzhard, and toe vice-chair was occupied by P.P.G.M. Burrows, C.S. of the district. A number of toasts were proposed. " Success to the Manchester Unity of Odd- follows" was proposed by the vice-chairman, who coooletl with the toast the name of the chairman. He said he had been an Oddfellow for 20 years and had passed through many of the positions in the district. He became more and more convinced of the soundness of their order, and its ability to fulfil the obligations and contracts entered into with those initiated. Their Unity took the lead of friendly sr•cieties, and there vras not a shadow of a doubt that by joining it the members' were made better citizens.

In responding, Bro. John Pritchard said he was ever ready and ever willing to help forward the great mighty and noble institution—the Mancnester Unity of Oidfellews of which they were waits]. It had implanted and fostered in the minds of its members that true spirit i of self-reliance and self-dependence, which was the basis of their order. To such an extent had the claims of friendly societies taken possession of the people of the United Kingdom that they found,whereas in France only one person in 78, and in Belgium only one in 64 were as- sociated with them, in this country the proportion wa s one in nine. When they came to look at the fact that so large a number of workingmen had spontaneously organised themselves into voluntary associations for support in times of sickness and distress, they must admit that they had a tremendousinfluence for good on the well-being of the nation at large. The speaker pro- ceeded to sp.ak of the history of friendly societies from the year 1613, when a number of Scotchmen formed the " Scottish Box," to the present time, and stated that it was not until the year 1793 that they were recognised by the Government. An Act was then passed for the protection of their funds by allowing them to invest their savings in Government securities. Allud- ing to the Manchester Unity of Odrlfellows, he said it was established in the year 1312. Tile society now held the foremost position among the friendly societies of the land. Their membership was 722,723, and the ac- cumulated capital £8.420,452 43. 6d., or about £ll 13;. per member. In 1893 the amount paid for sick benell; was £662,736 193. 2d., and the funeral tx.riefits £140,297 13s. 91. The bare statement of those figures showed that the power produced by friendly societies . could not fail to have a very important influence upon the well-being and-liberties of the people of this countrç. lie trusied the members would continue their efforts in securing the further prosperity of their Order. He then prooosed the toast "Success to the Past Grand Lodge." P.P.G.M. Wright responded, and mentioned that the pact grand lodge was simply an auxiliary lodge, and the benefits received from it were additional irom the respective societies of which they were members. He stated that thrA lo tee under whose auspices they were net was i in 1837, and Sirtee then £230 had heen paid doe sick, and £35 tor funerals, and the total expenses including the skims spimt on their annual re-unions came to £402 12s. 91. f heir membership was 46, and the total worth of the Lodge £5l 133. 2. or £l_2s. Bid. per member.

The rematncler of the evening was spent in a convivial' manner.

WARR IN UTON WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL UNION.

The first meeting of the newly-formed Warrington Wesleyan Circuit Sunday School Uni.n was held at Bold-st. Wesleyan schoolroom, on Wednesday evening. Previous to the meeting, about 60 persons sat down to a substantial tea. After the tea, a number of reports of the various Sunday schools in the circuit which had been visited by appointed visitors, were read by the Rev. G. D. Mason, and showed that the majority of the schools w:•re in a very flourishing conditi,,n. A short discussion ensued, in which varicus points raised in the reports were discussed. The time, however, was too short for a proper discussion to take place,and the Union would do well to spend another night solely for this purpose.

After this preliminary meeting, a public meeting wan held, to hear an address from the Rev. Robert Culley, of London general secretary of the Wesleyan Sanday School Union. The chair was occupied by Councillor Shaw-Green. who expressed his pleasure at seeing so many present who had taken so much interest in Sunday school work. He was sure that they would receive much information and encour- agemerst from Mr. Culley's address. The Rev. ROBEZT CUL= then gave an address up- on "Discipline, and its possible improvement." At the conunencement of his address, Mr. Culley laid great stress upon the teaching of the catechism in the Sunday school. The Roman Catholics, the members of the Es— tablished Church, and the Presbyterians were very strict in teaching the catechism, and he thought that In that respect, the Methodists would do well to copy them.. Coming to the subjects of Sunday schools, he said that the weakest place in their armour as Sunday school teachers was the discipline of the school. They were very strong in numbers, hut he must confess, niey were not very strong in discipline. There was no institution in the country which exerted such a wholesome influent:* as the Sunday school, and therefore he thought that this ought not to be. He would make a fair suggestion, and give a few causes why this state of things occurred. In Sunday school it was iinpossible to have absolute quietude, but still the opening exercise should be con- ducted with perfect decorum. They could get this if they were determined to keep it. When the scbolare were dismissed to the classrooms, there should not be a smote of confusion. There should also be abso- lute stillness during the singing of the closing hymn,and address, and the elder scholars sh mild also be in the school. As to the causes of disorder, he might say that there was not the same power behind a Sunday school teacher as there was behind a day school teacher. A day school teacher had the stick (laughter), and there was also the law of the land behind him. The Sunday school teacher had not this power behind him. Dual control in the schools was also frequently a cause of ' disorder. It sometimes happened that there were several superintendents, whose mode of govern- ment varied. This caused the scholars to be unsettled, and thus contributed to the disorder. Too rapid moving up of scholars had much to do with it. He thought that the scholars should be under one teacher much longer than they were at some schools. Weak teachi,.; also contributed to Poor order. An in- teresting teacher always had a good class. Indiscrimin- ate giving of rewards also weAtened the authority of the teachers. No scholar should receive a prize for good attendance, if his conduct should been The- closing address of schools should not used as the practice of inexperienced boys, •it the ,1 speaker should be an interesting one. To ker, order, the teachers should be at school a few moments before time, to get order at the commencement ef the schoolr The scholars should each have a hymn-book, , and thus take part in the opening hymn. The superintenden. should not be afraid to Call out the name of a bad scholar, and cliques should always be broken up at once. The teachers should also visit the scholars them- selves. Ih conclusion, he did not think that the scholars.. would stop away from school if good discipline was kept, but that they would have an increased.respeet for it.

fhe meeting was brought to a cloce by the ucual votaa of tanks, and the pronouncing of the tvnedietion.

Accord inff tn the latest reports, there are in the world 140,334 milea of .uh-marine telegraph cable. Of this total the various Governments own 14,480 miles of cable and 21,550 miles of wire : the balance is owned by private companies. Here is an analysis of what is alleged to be the typical American face :—The prominent nose, the sloping fore- head, the fairly large mouth, the full eyes, and the pre- domineoce of the oval type are the natural characteris- tics of an aggressive, ta.lented, and shrewd people, agreeable in manners, but keenly alive to the main chance. It is a composite face, made up of qualities taken from Puritan, English, Scotch, and German sources.

HOW TO DEFEA.7 OUR ENEMIES

Everything that tends to weaken our constitution an render it liable to disease is an enemy of mankind, and everything that tends to withstand the attacks of these enemies and to restore health and strength to the human system is a true friend. Among the most prominent and most dreadful, like so many generals in the attacking orces of the great enemy of mankind—death—may be mentioned the following :—lndieestion, liver complaint, nervous disorders, and chest affections, while the differ- ent symptoms by which these diseases manifest them- selves may be regarded as so many sub-officers in the ranks of the enemy. When any one of these attacks us we may be certain that some of the others are close at band. The stomach, liver, lungs, nerves, and blood, are portions of the body that the enemy is very prone to assail, and if he succeeds in maintaining his position for any length of time in any one of them, and is not quickly expelled, he soon extends his pernicious domin- ions to other parts of the body. When the stomach is dis- ordered, we may be certain that the enemy in some form or other has gained a foothold there, and if the food he not properly digested, the blood does not receive a suffi- ciency of the vital particulars necessary to diffuse through all puts of the system in order to afford proper nourishment to the body. In our climate, affections of the chest are very prevalent in the form of colds, coughs bronchitis, asthma. &e. NEWTON PETTY SESSIONS. SATIIRDAY.—Before Mr. T. J. Gdlespie (chairman), Mr. T. H. Pearson, Mr. R. Stone, and Colonel J. L.

Wood. SHOCKING NEGLECT OF FARlLY.—Elizabeth Travis was charged with neglecting her family on October Ist in a manner likely to cause them grievous bodily harm. Mr. J. W. Knowles appeared on behalf of the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children. He stated that the defendant had been summoned to attend a previous court, but she had absconded. In the mean- time she had been arrested at Warrington on the charge of drunkenness and had just served a term of two months. He (Mr. Knowles) asked for a severe penalty to be inflicted as from the evidence which would be adduced, the bench would see that there had been gross ! neglect practised by the defendant.—Inspector McAteer' N.S.P.C.C. stated that on October Ist, he visited the house of the defendant, 75, Church-street, Golborne, in I company with P.C. Simpson. They found the defendant and her children—Peter, 10 years; Patrick, eight years; Mary, six years; James, four years; Bridget, two years; and Elizabeth, six weeks—in the kitchen, in a most deplorable condition, without foscl or fire. He exam- ined the children, and found them in a most filthy con- dition. The shirt which Peter wore, appeared never to have been washed. All the children were covered with body lice, and on moving the clothing, some of the lice actually dropped to the floor. The beds and furniture were also in a wretched condition, and there were scarcely any bedclothes. The smell in the house was al- most unendurable. On the 13th October witness again visited the house, and then found the woman almost helplessly drunk. The children were in the same condi- tion as on the first visit. Upon remonstrating with the defendant she lifted up a chair, and smashed it before their eyes. The defendant had not been brought before the bench earlier, because she had absconded when she received a summons. She was subsequently brought before the Widnes magistrates for some offence, and she then stated she had travelled all over South and North Wales, and Cheshire,'and had been without employment for nine months, which was not true. The magistrates discharged the woman on condition that she would go home. The same night she went to Warrington with her children and became drunk. She commenced beat- ing and kicking Peter in Buttermarket-st. Seeing who she was, witness gave her in charge,and the next morn- ing she was brought before Mr. Garnett, at the War- rington Police Court on the charge of drunkenness. Mr. Garnett on giving the decision of the Bench in the case, said that the prisoner must go to gaol for two months, but had it not been that she was wanted at Newtnn,they would have given her six months.—Dr. Part, Golborne gave corroborative evidence, and said that the condi- tion of the children was worse, if anything, than the Inspector had stated. The children were nourished,but that was through the kindness of Father Bird,lwho took them food, and washed them.—Mr. Gillespie said that the case was painful and disgusting, and the tench would order the prisoner to go to gaol for three months, which was the highest term they could inflict. CLarat AGAINST A FRIENDLY SOCIETY.—James Dear- den, collier, made a claim against the British General Friendly Society, for the premium upon the policy of Annie Ilazelden, who had died in November. Mr. J. P. Mearns, solicitor, St. Helens, represented the plaintiff and the Society was represented by the general secretary, Mr. Turnbull, of Birmingham. Mr. Mearns, in opening the case said that the present 'case was one which the plaintiff lied the privilege of taking either to the County ' Court, or the court of Summary Jurisdiction. and it was sometimes more convenient that the plaintiff should adopt the latter course. James Dearden was a collier, residing at Woodland Terrace, Haydock. He was per- suaded to join the Society above mentioned, and loin- sure his sister, Annie Hazelden, for £l2 18s.. at 61. per week. The policy was issued in June, 1893. The pre- miums were regularly paid until Mrs. Flazelden died in Nov. 1894. Dearden had then paid £1 Si. He then claimed the policy, but the local Secretary came to his house and said that the Society were not prepared to pay the policy at once, but would give him £1 on account. Dearden replied that he would only receive £1 on accsiunt on condition that the balance was paid to him. Dearden could not read nor write, and he then put a cross to a document which he understood was a receipt for the £1 on account. The document was not read over to him. A short time afterwards he was in- formed by Mr. Price Lewis that the Society refuse to pay any more. Dearden then consulted him (Mr. Mearns), and proceedinge were at once instituted.— Dearden then gave evidence in support of Mr. Mearn's statement. In reply to Mr. Turnbull the pliintiff said that he was not aware that Mrs. Hazelden was ouffering from gastric catarrh at the time the policy was taken out. No (lector came to examine her.—Esidence to show that Dearden only signed the receipt for £1 on the condition that he received the balance,was given by Mrs. Dresden, and a son-in-law, and stepson.—Mr. Turnbull said that the society's case was that Mrs, Hazelden was represented to thetsociety as being in a sound state of health when the policy was taken out, She was also stated to be free from any of the diseases which are enumerated upon a form issued by the society. It was not the custom to send a doctor for Small insurances. After the policy had been taken out, the premiums were paid more or less regularly lentil Mrs. Flazelden's death, but upon the certificate of death being examined it was disccvered that the deceased had been suffering from gastric catarrh for three years. This was not an I insidious disease, but a very painful one, 'and plaintiff must have been aware of it when he took out the policy. When this was discovered, the society offered to pay defendant £l' which would he a loss of 3d. to the society after deducting the cost of collection. Price Lewis and Job Whitehouse two servants of the society would state that the document which plaintiff signed was read over to him before he signed it, and there- fore that he was aware of what he was signing.—Evid- ence to this effect was given by the two above mentioned eentlemin.—The bench decided in favour of the plaintiff with COOS. WARRINGTON COUNTY PErrY SESSIONS. WBDNESDAY.—Before Major Pick mere (chairman), and Mr. J. Crosfield. A SLEEPY DRIVER.—John Rimtner, Rixton, was fine 2s. 61 and costs, for being asleep while in charge of a horse and lurry in Rixton. DRUNK. AND DIORDERLY.—For drunkenness George Walsby, Croft, was fined ss. and costs ; Peter Moss, Sankey, ss. and costs. No NAME ON CART.—James Simcock was charged with using his cart in Manchester-road, Woolston, on January 4th, without having his name painted upon it. Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins who represented the defendant, pleaded guilty, and explained that the cart was on sale and was only used temporarily, and, therefore, Mr. Simcock had put the name in chalk on the cart. Of course this was wrong, but it was done in ignorance of the law, and the defendant was prepared to pay costs. The magistrates agreed upon this offer. ALLEGED POLLUTION OF SEWERS.—Charles Holland,' Richard Graham, and Messrs. Kelly Bros., all of Rixton, were charged with not ceasing to turn sewage into the drains, in Bank-st., Rixton, on October 13th, 1391, Mr. A. G. Steel, barrister, Liverpool defended.—Frank Ker- foot, nuisance inspector for the Warrington Rural Dis- trict Council, said that the proceedings were taken under Sections 95 and 96 of the Public Health Ant. On October 13th, a notice was served upon the defendant to abate a nuisance caused by the drainage of their houses being turned into a drain which was formerly a water course. The watercourse a short distance further down entered upon land in the occupation of market gardeners, who had to wash their vegetables for the market in it. Not only that, but it was also used for drinking purposes for cattle,and their milk has been so bad that it had to be thrown away. The drains of the water closets of the house of Messrs. Kelly Bros., also drained into the water coume, and during last summer it had been most offensive.—Mr. A. G. Steel said that he did not disputa the facts of the case at all, so that he did not think it necesrary to go into details. According to cases which had been de- cided, it was laid down that a sewer had a very wide meaning, and that if a watercourse received the drains of several houses it became thereby a sewer By the proceedings which were taken that morning, the War- ringtun Sanitary Authority were endeavouring to get out of the obligation which was imposed upon them by the Public Health Act. They were responsible for the drainage of their district, and it was they who ought to lay down proper sewers. They had also, by Act of Parliament, full control over the sewer in question, as that Act referred to, vested all existing sewers in them. He also contended that after the sewage had left the houses, the occupants were not in the least responsible for any nuisance is might cause—. Mr. Keifoot said that the houses were built before the passing of the Public Health Act, and therefore the Sanitary Authority had then no control of the construction.—Mr. W. Owen, sur- veyor to the Warrington Rural District Council, also addressed the Bench. He said that had the houses been , built at the present day, they would not have been passed. If a drainage scheme were adopted to include those houses, it would entail a eost of £2,000 upon the Authority. The houses were not in a neighbourhood with an increasing popula- tion. Dr. Sephton, medical officer to the Authority, said that here was great danger of fever from the state af the drains, and he could not answer for the consequences if it were allowed to remain Cesspools might be formed, and the sevroge could be used up in many ways.—Mr.Steel said that this did not do away with his contention that the defendants were not responsible. In fact, Dr. Sephton was making a strong case against the Sanitary Authority, who were responsible.—The magistrates dismissed the case. VAGRANCY.—James Whittle was sent to gaol for 14 days for sleeping in a hut belonging to Lewis Brookes, Woolston, on January 15, and having no visible means of subsistence. The evidence showed that the prisoner had broken a window in order to get into the hut, where he was found asleep.

"ONCE UPON A TIME."

Ah ! what memories, hopes, sad-fated, Scene-shifts in Life's pantomime— With this phrase associated : Once upon a time" ! See yon old man, shrunken, weary, Toddling on in saddest plight, Bent, his form, his eyes are bleary— What a piteous sight ! Time was when he trode so sprightly— Straight, robust in manhood's prime—. Wild fandangoes danced he lightly, " Once upon a time." There's old Purstiff, anxious, dumpy, Now Love's follies doth taboo— Shuts his daughters up, so grumpy. Lest young men,them woe. Ah ! the nights he spent love-trysting, Full of Love's delights sublime ; Oft on " stolen kisses" feasting, " Once upon a time !" There's the old maid," strict " as could be, Urging st*rongly as she can, On young lasses that they should be— Ware of "wicked man." Who could think she loved with rapture, Wrote a lot of amorous rhyme, Arts and wiles used " man " to capture, "Once upon a time" ! We, in mid-age, find Life's progress Dull, monotonous, and drear, Hope's fair goddess an old ogress, Joy's outcome—a tear. Ah ! how bright Life's dome shone o'er 113 ; What grand fairs- heights we'd climb; Love. ambition, fame before us—, " Once upon a time " ! enmitiPs, divorces. Much that lead to strife and grime— Love and Friendship had for sources, "Once upon a time."

MIRACLE AT A RECTORY. !mg! STRANGE OCCURRENCE IN SUFFO K.

Three or four months back we told (says the "Essex Telegraph ") the story of the miraculous cure of a young woman of Great Bentley, whose friends had despaired of her life. A second case, which a represen- tative of the " Essex Telegraph " investigated more re- cently, confirms what was reported en that occasion. A new and wonderfully simple every-day remedy has been found, different and apart from ordinary medi- cine, and remarkably efficacious in very diverse diseases. The "Telegraph" reporter called at Brant- ham Rectory, and saw Mrs. Emily Youngs, who at once confirmed certain rumours of her extraordinary re- covery which had provoked the reporter's visit. "Thank God," said she, "I can work and sleep now, and if there are any who suffer as I did, I shall only be too glad to tell them how to get relief." " In the beginning of 1894," Mrs. Youngs continued, "I was attacked with rheumatic pains in the arm, se severe that it became practically useless. I could not, work; I could not sleep ; I could not raise my hand to my head, and had to get assistance in dressing. I tried embrocations, but got no relief, and then I had the advantage of careful attention from a skilled doctor, and went to Ipswich for treatment, but came back no better, and feeling quite unable to carry out my housekeeping duties, I made arrangements to relinquish them. Just then I read in one of the local papers of a wonderful cure effected by the aid of Dr. Williams' Pills for Pale People, and resolved to give them a trial. After taking one box I was:much better. I bought a second box, and by the time I had got half way through its contents I was quite well. My arm had completely recovered, and I was also feeling better in health generally, than I had done for many months. The cure was complete, and I think Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are a splendid medicine. I am able now to work, and to carry out my household duties with pleasure. There are many poor soffering creatures who ought to know that they can find relief in Dr. Williams' Pink Pills." PARR'S BANKING COMPANY AND THE ALLIANCE BANK LIMITED.

ANNUAL REPORT. The following report of the Directors of Parr's Bank- ing Company and the Alliance Bank Limited, will be submitted to the Proprietors at the 30th annual ordinary general meeting of the Comnany, to be held at the Cannon-at. Hotel, London, E.C., on Thursday, the 24th of January :—" The Directors, in submitting to the shareholders the balance sheet a the Bank at 31st Dec., 1894, certified by the auditors, have to report that the position and prospects of the business continue to be in every way satisfactory. Including £34,628 3s. 10d. brought forward at the 30th June, 1894, the net balance of profits, after providing amply for all bad and doubt- ful debts, and rebating the current bills at 5 per cent. is £135,031 95. 3d., from this sum the directors recom- mend the payment of a dividend at the rate of 19 per per cent, per annum, free of income tax, for the quarter ended 31st December. 1894, to be payable on Ist Feb., 1895, £47,500. Dividend at the same rate f.-,T the quar- ter ending 31st March, 1895, to be payable on Ist May, 1895, to the shareholders whose names shall be on the register on 18th April, £47,500—£95,000 ;—to Bank' premises account, £5,000—£100.000 ; leaving balance to be carried to next account, £35,031 9s. 3d. During the past six months the directors have acquired the old established business of Messrs. Croxon, Jones and Co. (Old Bank), Limited, Oswestry. Mr. F. G. Buller Swete one of the Directors of that Company has con- sented' to act as local director. A suls-branch has been established at Standish, near Wigan. The directors who retire at this time by rotation are Mr. A. J. Fraser, Mr. N. if. Eckerslay, Hon. W. St. J. F. Brodrick, M.P., Mr. William Dunn, M.P., Mr. H. B. Muir, Rt. Hon. Lord Playfair, K.C.8., and Mr. H. C. Ross, all of whom are eligible, and are recommended for re-election. The auditors, Messrs. Turquand, Youngs,l3ishop and Clarke, London, and Messrs. Stead Taylor and Stead Liverpool, also retire, and offer themselves for re-election,"

RUNCORN FREE LIBRARY

COMMITTEE.

-- The first meeting of the newly-formed Free Library Committee at Runcorn was held on Tuesday evening in the Board-room. There were present : Messrs. William Handley. S. Pope, T. Riley. and J. D. Jones (librarian). THE CHAIRMANSHIP AND VICE-CHAIRMANSHIP. Mr. S. Pope moved that Mr. William Handley be ap- pointed the chairman of the committee. Mr. Riley seconded the nroposition which was carried unani- mously.—Mr. Grice was appointed vice-chairman. It was decided that the time of meeting should be altered from 8 o'clock to 8.15 p.m. THE BOOK SELECTION COMMITTEE. It was resolved that all the members of the Free Library Committee should cempose the abolie-named meeting as heretofore.

FINANCE. The financial statement- submitted showed that the receipts amounted to £35 N. 43. as follows :—Balance in hand, £34 54. 6d. ; fines, lie. ; tickets, Ils. Bd. ; and catalogues, is. 2d. The payments were—salaries, £5 ; new books, £6 ss. ; magazines and newspapers, £8 6s. sd. ; fittings, £6 O. 6d. ; leaving a balance in hand of £9 17s. sd. OFFER BY MR. BRUNNIIR, M.P. A letter was read from Mr. Sutton, bookseller, Deans- gate, Manchester, intimating that Mr. Brunner, M.P., had instructed him to forward at his expense a copy of Orturod's "History of Cheshire," if the Library Committee were not in possession of a copy. The Librarian stated that the committee had the book in the library. It was decided to thank Mr. Brunner for his offer.

THE HALF-HOLM TALKS. The Librarian was instructed to thank, on behalf of the committee. Mr. Wilding and Mr. Ogle for their lectures, and also the Chairman at the half-hour talks in the library on books and their authors. It was stated that the lectures had proved useful in introducing cer- tain books to readers. THE MONTHLY STATEMENT. The Librarian's statement showed that the total num- ber of books issued during the month of December was 1833 in the lending department. and 49 in the reference department,making a total of 1882. This was an'increase of three in the lending derrirtibent, and a decrease of eight in the reference department as compared with the corresponding menth of the previous year. The books issued in the lending department were as follows : Theology 28, philosophy 14, science 74, history 137, poetry 16, fiction 1381, miscellaneous 183. The follow- ing gifts were reported : By Mr. John Simpson " Memoirs of Libraries " 2 vols.; and Miall's British Churches and the British people. By Mr. Walton Milling- ton, Tebh's " Recrudescence of leprosy and its causa- tion." Bradford Free Library report.

RUNCORN PARISH CHURCH TEA MEETING.

THE VICAR ON BOARD SCHOOLS. Runcorn Parish ghurch congregational tea party was held in the National Infants School on Monday evening. There was a large attendance at tea, the tables being presided over by the following ladies :—Mrs. Wood, Miss White, Misses Smith, Mrs. Sutton, Miss Bate, Misses , Timmins, Mrs. Gregory, Mrs. James Littler, lilies John- son, Mrs. Abel, Mrs. W. Jones, Mrs. Collier, Mrs. J. S. Davies, Mrs. McLennan, Mrs. Holden, Mrs. Hamilton, Mrs. Crosby, Mrs. A. Salkeld, Mrs. Briscow, Mrs. W. B. Kay and Mrs. Anderton. After tea, the room was prepared for the entertain- ment, at which the Rev. Canon Maitland Wood, M.A. (vicar), presided. A programme of vocal and instru- mental music was submitted and greatly appreciated by the audience. Misses Comerford and Simkin and Messrs. Littler and Branch formed a quartette party, and well rendered several pieces during the evening. Solos were sung by Miss May Clare, Miss Simkin, Miss Comerford, Mr. Branch, Mr. Pownall, Mr. H. Clare, and Mr. Wilkinson. Mr. F. L. Scott contributed a humorous song, and duets were given by Messrs. Littler and Branch, and Misses Clare and Simkin. Mr. Cyril Chantler quite delighted the audience with his recital of 'Captain Norris," and in response to vociferousapPlause gave another recitation, entitled, " How Rubinstein played the piano." Mr. R. Birkett Musgrove. organist at Frodsham Parish Church, was the accompanist dur-

lug the evening. During an interval, the Rev. Canon Wood delivered an address. At the outset he referred to several changes which had taken place during the year. At the begin- ning of the year they were all exceedingly sorry to lose Mr. Montgomery, whose place had been taken by Mr. Firth, and they would all agree with him that it would have been difficult to get anyone more accept- able. There was another change about to take place. They were about to lose Mr. Holden, and although they were sorry that he was leaving them, yet they were very glad that he had been promoted to be vicar of Antrobus. (Applause.) Mr. Holders had been amongst them almost seven years. He was not going until after Easter, when he would have completed seven—the perfect number—of years amongst them. tie did not think Mr. Holden had made himself one enemy,but was sure he had madetimany friends. He was unablo to tell them who was going to take Mr. Hal- den's place as he did not know. There had been a great many changes in their schools. The speaker then referred to the promotion of Mr. Lindsey, Mr. D. S. Garner, Mr. Rhodes, and Miss Highfield,who had been engaged in their day schools. They had now a number of new teachers, who, he was sure, would keep the re- putation as high as ever. It had been to him a source of the greatest pleasure and satisfaction to be able to feel that an addition to their schools had been made. He thought it was three or four years ago since he ven- tured to indicate to them that an enlargement to their schools was necessary. It was then met with a great deal of incredulity, but when H.M. Inspector came down he told them that their schools were too crowded, and that they could not take as large a number of chil- dren in future. The res :It was the enlarge n ent of the schools, and the rooms would be ready for the reception of girls in a few days. They had spent from £450 to £5OO in making the alterations. He felt perfectly con- fident that the voluntary school system was going to re- main much longer than some people thought it would do. He had no desire of reflecting on the Runcorn School Board,the Board schools,or Board school teachers. or anything connected with the Runcorn Board. He had the greatest respect for the headmaster of the Runcorn Board schools. He was glad to 'say that the Board did their work with the greatest economy possible, consistent with efficiency. But he wished to call their attention to the fact that the Board schools had got the rate. Having rates, which were almost bottomless purses to dip into, the School Boar.l were able to give the best education. They offer, I larger salaries to the teachers than could be ' offer.-1 by the voluntary schools, and were thus able to draw from the free schools the best teachers. He did not t lame the teachers, but when they had taken them away, people sang out in the newspapers how much bett-r Board schools were than voluntary schools. They were told that their voluntary schools only earned a government grant of 18s. id. per child, whereas the Board schools had got 18s. 11d. But what was the difference paid in salaries ? Well, in voluntary schools the salaries paid averaged a little more than £1 83. per child, whereas the Board schools paid £1 175., so that they spent 93. extra in salaries, and at the examination they earned Ild. or 10d. But there were some people who had a perfect mania for upsetting the voluntary schools and putting Board schools in their places. They thought they could make the education of children much better all the world over by Board schools. If they were all Board schools then it would be only one Board bidding against another for the best teachers. .fhere was this curious little point—if the voluntary schools were given up there would be tremendous ex- pense for building. He supposed he might use the illus- tration they had in the town. Their Wesleyan friends, for good reasons or for bad, had made up their minds that they would not maintain their voluntary schools. The consequence was that the Board was at its wits end to provide a new school. The cost to build a school averaged about £l2 for the accommodation of one child, but he did not suppose the lioncorn Board would spend more than £lO per child. But if they spent that much it would mean that £7,000 would be taken out of that unhappy town to supply the place of the Wesleyan school, to say nothing of the additional expenses afterwards. He wondered whether any reasonable person could wish that to be repeated. He hoped they would grasp the situation fully. The care of the church people for the religious education of the children was saving the public a great deal of money. Every person who paid rates, whether they compounded them or not, would if those schools were closed, have a very considerable addition made to the rates. When they came before them, as they did once a year,for the support of their free schools, he con- fessed that he had been surprised to find such a very small response made. But they had been able to carry out their alterations at that school, and they had also got the money. He was not going to tell them where they got the money from, but there was one gentleman he wanted to thank publicly and that wee Mr. Edwin Imison who had helped him greatly in the work. The speaker then referred to the local authority charging them additional rates for the school, which he con- sidered was not fair play. It would he a good thing if the Authority would let that school and other free voluntary schools be free from rating. They eould de so if they wished, but if they did not, he was quite persuaded that there would be an Act passed by the lAgislature which would prevent them rating voluntary schools. Alluding to the distress in the town, the speaker said that from what he could learn from a pro- minent gentleman in the town, one of the Runcorn works had still got on hand the whole of a ceriain kind of their product which was made last year, and had not been able to dispose of any of it. He was afraid the town had to look forward to a difficult time,but he was I very glad that some people had beefi good enough to help those in need. They were able to distribute some- thing like £9O last week, but £9O would not Igo far when they had to assist hundreds of I people. He was sorry to , say that the most they could do at pr ,sent was to give a man and his wife and say six children, about seven shillings a week. Now was the time for men who were able to give money, to give it. Some of the child- ren came to ECIIOOI without any breakfast, and some without shoes to their feet. On one morning there were 113 children in the school who wanted some breakfast. The Chairman then spoke of the efforts being made to provide free breakfasts at the school. There were very many gentlemen connected with the town who did not live in Rlirlenftl, and perhaps were not aware of the distress.. Large fortunes had been made in Runcorn, and if those people had any regard for the place where they had made their money, they might help the people in distress at the present time. In addition to provid- ing fond,..they ought to be able to add. a certain quan- titr of coal. (Applause.; In eonclusion, the Vicar al- luded to the recent elections, stating that he now occu- pied a very proud position, having been elected chair- man of the Weston Parish Council, and a member of the Runcoon Board of Guardians.

• STARTLING INCIDENT AT A MEETING.

The Duke of Argyll attended a great Liberal Unionist ' gathering in Glasgow on Tuesday night, and after he had spoken for twenty minntes, and was describing the different kinds of Prime Ministers Great Britain had had in the past, he was seen tc change colour, his voice sank away to a whisper, and he fell with his head upon the deQk and then reeled into the arms of Lord Kelvin. There was intense excitement in the building, the duke's mouth being tight and his eyes closed. All of a sudden he awoke as if he had been dreaming,and stared around, , not seeming to realize where he was. After being at- tended to, he regained consciousness, and a motion of sympathy with him having been unanimwsly carried, the meeting dissolved.

SMITH'S TASTELESS DANDELION ANTIBILIOUS PILLS.

WARRINGTON ANGLERS' ASSOCIATION. ANNUAL MEETING. The annual meeting of the Warrington Anglers' As- sociation was held on Tuesday evening at the old Masonic rooms, Sankey-st., when the chair was occu- pied by the president, Mr. J. Mawsen, and he was also supported on the platform by Messrs. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk), W. H. J. Jenkins, and P. Knight (sec.) Before the business of the meeting commenoed, Mr. Knight read a letter from Mr. E. M. Crosfield, J.P.,who apologised for not being present that evening on ac- cotmt of a long standing engagement. He offered to stock:the River Gowy with Loch Leven trout,and asked the members' opinion upon the matter. Mr. JENKINS then explained that Mr. Mawson was very unwell and that he would not be able to say muoh to them that night. With reference to Mr. Crosfield's letter, he might explain that Mr. Mawson had resolved to retire from the position of president which he had occupied so long, and the committee had thereupon de- termined to recommend the name of Mr. E. M. Crosfield as his successor. Mr. Crosfield had promised to accept the position, and among other things he was prepared to stock the River Gewy, which the Society had obtained the fishing rights of,with Loch Leven trout. (Applause.) SECRETARY'S REPORT. Mr. P. Knionr,in presenting the annual report of the association, said that the past year had been a fairly successful one. Although the membership had decreased from 249 in 1893 to 193 for the past year, the income showed an improvement through the payment by hon. members of the higher scale of fees, and by private donations. They had concluded arrangements with the Earl of Shrewsbury and Talbot for the fishing in the River Gowy, and an agreement had been signed by which they had secured the whole of fishing rights on the Trafford estate at a yearly rental of £2O. They had not a very good report of the Lymm water except that a fair number of well-fed pike had been taken, the largest of which over 6ilbs., took the prize for the bast specimen. They regretted to report a very serime financial loss in connection with the exhibitien of fist and fishing; tackle held in the Gymnasium in June last. There was an excellent lot of exhibits, but for some reason it was not supported by anglers and friends, and it resulted in a loss of over £B. Two angling contests had been held during the year, one at Beeston Castle on August 23rd, and at North wich on August 25th. Owing to climatic conditions, there were only 17 competitors at the former, but there were 57 in the second. Eighteen prizes were given to the value. of £9 is. They had received a letter from the secretary of the Provincial Anglers' Association to the effect that the London and North-Western railway had granted the privilege of c'leap tickets to three stations, which was followed five on the Great Western rail- way, and four on the, Cambrian, making twelve as fol- lows :—Hartford, Minshull Vernon, Beeston Ca.stle,Dun- ham Hill, Rossett, Cefn, Llangollen, Corwen, Llanymy- nech, Four Crosses, Pooi Quay, and Buttington, at t'm rate of about single fare and a quarter for the double journey. There were also 20 branches of the Provincial Association, at any of which places the members on tour could book to any of the 143 stations to which the places had access, oa production of travelling cards. Warrington branch had now the right of being repre- sented on the Provincial Association. There was much to be done during the ensuing season, and with the ad- vantages of cheap travelling, better prospects of angl- ing in Association waters, &c., they hoped to enlist the support of all anglers and friends in the district.—The report was adopted on the motion of Mr. Eusost, seconded by Mr. PORTER. Mr. KNIGHT also read the financial statement, which showed that the year commenced with a balance of £3 189. 3d.* The income during the year had been £52 15s. Bd., and the expenditure £5O 25., leaving a balance in favour of the society of £2 13s. 6d.—Tha report was adopted on the motion of Mr. BANCROFT, seconded by Mr. W. Y. LAWTON. ELECTION OF OFFICERS. Mr. J. MAWSON, who had been president of the Society for Some years. decided to retire, and on the motion of Mr. Jenkins, seconded by Councillor Baird, Mr. E. M Crosfield, J.P., was re-elected president for the ensuing year. On the motion of Mr. W. Y. Lawton, seconded by Mr. Bancroft, the following gentlemen were appointed vice-presidents : Messrs. J. Lyon Whittle, W. H. J. Jen- kins, J. L. Tunstall, Spencer Wallhead,E, Milner, Major Ridgyvay,Councillors Heaton and Greening. E. Caldwell, W. Skelton,A. Lister Kaye, J. Mawson, T. Mottram,W. A. Proud. W. Podmore, S. Hartshorne, R. Hamblin, W. Taylor, R. Hendry, W. Bolton, W. Wood, and Rev. T. Pym The following gentlemen were elected as a commit- tee : Messrs. J. Lyon Whittle, W. H. J. Jenkins, J. Mawson. T. J. Emson, C. Hesitater, A. Cooke, H. Kent, J. Bancroft, R. Hendry, E. P.)rter, W. Bodle, J. Bailey, and W. Wood. Mr. Peter Knight was re-elected secretary, on the motion of Mr.J.Lyon-Whittle,seconded by Mr.Wood,and supported by Mr. A. Cooke. Mr. W. Bolton was re-elected treasurer, on the motion of Mr. Jenkins. seconded by Mr. J. Roberts. Mr. Peter Knight was appointed delegate, and Mr. Jenkins deputy,to the annual Imeeting of the Provincial Anglers' Association, held at Birmingham. At the close of the meeting a hearty vote of thanks was passed to Mr. Mawson for his services as president, on the motion of Mr. Bodle, seconded by Mr. Hoop.

TEIE INQUEST.

- - Mr. J. E. Woraley, coroner, held an inquest at Runcorn Police Station on Wednesday morning relative to the death of a child, three years and six months old. named Peter Johnson, son of Joseph Nichols Johnson, of 6. Back King-street, Runcorn.—Mary Johnson, mother of the deceased, said her husband was a chemical works labourer. On gaturday, about 6.20 in the evening, she went out of the house to do shopping, leaving the de- ceased and his brother, one year and six months old, in the kitchen. There was a good fire in the kitchen when she left them. She was from ten to fifteen minutes away. On her return he found deceased in her next door neighbour's house, suffering from burns. Dr. McKenzie had then been sent for, and he attended de- ceased until his death at 4.0 p.m. on Sunday. Deceaed told her that the fire had burnt him, but did not tell her how it happened. She was of opinion that the deceased must have been standing too Near' to the fire, and his clothes thus become ignited. There was no one in the house at the time except the two children.—Luke McDonald said he lived next door to the last witness. On Saturday night about 6.20, witness and another: man named Kelly heard screams and ran into Johnson's house and found the deceased in flames. Kelly stripped the clothes off the child. He could not say how the accident happened.—Michael Kelly corro- borated the evidence of the last witness, stating that he badly blistered his hand whilst stripping the gar- ments off the deceased.—Dr.'iMcKenzie said be wos sent for to see the deceased child on Saturday. He found it suffering from severe burns from the head to the middle of the chest, and also on the back. He attended the child until its death on Sunday afternoon. It died from injuries and shock.—The Coroner said he did not think there was any blame attached to the mother fcr leaving the children. There wag some commendation due to the neighbours for the way they tried to save the life of the child, especially to the man who got his hand burnt in extinguishing the illanlee.-I."yetclict of "Accidental death" wee returned.

BBWRIT STREET, WA/IRINOTON

MR. COUNCILLOR PERCY DAVIES AND THE WATCH COMMITTEE. Sir,—Referring to one of your "editorials," which appeared in your last Saturday's issue, I may say, I at- tended the meeting of the Town Council on the Bth instant, at considerable inconvenience to myself, in the hope that Mr. Davies would have been present, and that I should therefore have had an opportunity of telling him, that the allegation he made at the provious meet ing of the Counail, against the Watch Committee for having "stopped the proceedings against a certain 'respectable inhabitant,' charged with committing a l serious offence seainst the law, for which a poor person would have been severely punished," was entirelyuntrue. The fact being, that ne proceedings of any kind, were ever taken at all, against the individual in question! , therefore I cannot see hcw the Watch Committee could , have" stopped proceedings" which had never been taken (perhaps Mr. Davies can explain this enigma?) But Mr. Davies accentuated his charge by offering to give "Chapter and versa" in support of it. I fully intended to have told Mi. Davies, what I tell him now, that there was nvt a scintillation of truth in the absurd statement made. It would have been such an easy matter for him to have ascertained the whole truth, either by appealing to the Chief Constable, Mr. Hunt, or to the much abused " Watch Committee," and that, I naturally thought Mr Davies would have done, aid, finding that he had been misled, have made the " Amende honorable " to the ' Watch Committee for his rash and unfounded state- ment. I cannot of course believe, that Mr. Davies - mid nave invented it, nor can I believe that he r o wil- lingly have given pain to innocent peraons(whi a has done), by reviving that, which, whether tly or wrongly compromised, should have been Lt • ried "to the tomb of all the Capulets," but afraid Mr. Davies did not take the least trouble to cm himself of the true fazts of the case, before g via ins own ver- sion currency, and why he did not do so,i a one of those anomalies of " poor human nature," which (in the lan- guage of the late Lord Dundreary) " no fella can understand," hut if he had taken that prudent course, he would have found that neither Mr. Hunt, the Chief Constable, nor the Watch Clmsnittee, had ever received any official information whatever, until at least a week after the event had taken place, and then only by way of rumour from irresponsible persons, which Mr. Hunt found from inquiry to be wholly unreliable, for he could not obtain one single witness to come forward in support. I therefore now challenge Mr. Percy Davies to do what he voluntarily offered to do, at the Council meeting of I the 4th December, viz., to give" chapter and verse" in proof of his assertion, and if he can't do so, then I sub- mit, he is in honour bound to admit it, in a suitable apology to the Watch Committee for his ill advised and damaging statement. I have been myself chairman,and deputy chairman of the Watch Committee, for a good number of years, and lam not disnosed to sit still under such a serious and scandalous imputation, as that to which Mr. Davies has given tongue, and I say further, that if the Watch Committee could have acted in the way described by him it would have proved itself utterly unworthy to govern and control the police force, or any longer to merit the confide.ce of the public. The dan- ger of coming to hasty and one-sided conclusions,should be guarded against, by disclaiming any pretension to infallibility of statement in detail, or we may awake to the unpleasant facts, of wading through a " morass," in which we are ooly too likely to lose ourselves, in groping after a " Will-o'-th'-Wisp."—l am, sir, yours faithfully, JOSEPH HARRISON,

boasts that the main body of the seed remains un- changed " although the germ had quickened." Why did Mr. Peers supply his plant with sugar ? Because it had nothing to live on, for he had robbed the plant of its natural food, by taking away the main body of the seed which would have become sugar had it been left within the influence of the plant. Do farmers use sugar in the cultivation of wheat ? I have never heard of such a thing. Yet wheat would not grow without it Dow then is sugar supplied ? By the action of growth which converts the starch of the seed to sugar. Thus we see that the plant first takes away the life ' of the seed and then devours its material. Mr. Peers made a fuss over a part of my last letter which said " that if the seed did not die what would happen? Why, it would continue to throw out new plants." Ile in ridicule asks, whoever beard ot seed continuing to throw out"new plants? And lin my turn ask, whoever heard of a seed which did not die. Never- I theless there is a plant called couch grass (Triticum.' repens), which is a regular plague to farmers and gardeners. It has a long running root under the ground which behaves just like a never dying seed may be sup- posed to behave, for it continues to throw up new plants by,which its vigour is increased. Sow a piece of this an inch long, and if unmolested it will soon occupy a field. A Mr. Flallett, at Brighton, made some experiments ' some years since with wheat, in which he succeeded in raising eighty stalks from one crown of roots, formed from a single grain. One of the ears measured /3/ inches long and yielded! 123 grains. Now,when I read this I wanted to know what sort of an embryo didthese eighty stalks spring from ? I have always looked upon seed as possessing a potentiality rather than a bodily form—a Possibility that possessed the powers to realize itself. In conclusion, Mr. Peers' experiment is " infelicitous," slit r of instruction, and entirely misleading, deceiving n f elf and others. But lest he should think that I amt .king unjustly, I will spread the precious thing ou ft. s.amination. Get a cmn of wheat and cut out the germ erd place it on a damp cloth, and it will soon be giu the business of life, and, if supplied with sugar and some other non-living substances properly, it will grow to maturity. Please note—the main body of the corn seed is not in the experiment. If it had been in the ex- periment and survived, then it would have been clearly demonstrated that all I have written was fallacious, but as it was kept out for a purpose, and its absence bad to be made gocd by the use of sugar, it is a standing con- tradiction of Mr. Peers' theory, and makes all that I have said worthy of consideration. Let all who are in- terested in this subject get a few grains of wheat and sow them in the garden, or,failing a garden, some flower pots will do ; put two grains in a pot and when the plants have grown six inches high pull them up and then you will quickly see the condition of the seed as to its being alive or dead. The knowledge gained by this experiment admits of no doubt. The result if published would form a very good supplement to this discussion. —1 am, sir, yours truly, FELIX SUMMERS.

-- THE WARRINGTON DEBATING SOCIETY AND THE I.L.P. Sir,—Allow me a little space in your paper to make a few remarks on this subject. First, in your report last week you state the subject of debate was" The policy of the 1.L.P." It was advertised as " A Debate on Merrie England to be introduced by a member of the 1.L.P." As a matter of fact, Mr:Gee who introduced the subject never mentioned any socialistic: political party whatever and the fault of wandering from the subject rested with the subsequent speakers and the chairman for allowing them to do so. The I.L.P. is pre- pared at any time to debate and uphold its policy, but that was not the subject under discussiou. The real subject was Socialism as enunciated by " Nuaquana " in his book. In your notes by Merlin it is stated, "The I.L.P. was present in full force and speedily made their presence known by loudly cheering their own side and I am sorry to say, by hissing at the other side." By the way I notice Mr. Hall spoke in a similar manner. This, I take it implies that the majority present were I.L. Peers,and that it was they who interrupted. Now Sir, there were about thirty members of the I.L.P. there ' out of an audience numbering possibly a hundred and the first interruptions came from men who do not belong to our party, and in one case came from a member of the Debating Society itself. Most of the aulience not being accustomed to public speaking were not able to reply in the proper manner, and considering the provocation received from Mr. 'Torten were quite justified in their vigorous dissent. His speech contained what I consider direct insults to any body of workingmen and his manner was most offensive and dogmatic. In contrast to him Mr. Hall spoke some straight things, and it is worthy of note, was not interrupted once, because of his reasonable and argumentative manner. The debate was over too wide an area and it would be better to contract the subject a little on the next occasion which takes place I believe in February. Let me add, I write this at the wish and with the consent of the Executive of the Party.—l am, yours sincerely, J. PODMORE, jun., hon. sec.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

VOLUNTEER SMOKING CONCERT AT NEWTON. On Wednesday evening a smoking concert in connec tion with the Newton detachment of the ethV.B.K.L.R.- was held in the orderly room, Newton. Sergeant, Instructcr Bird prei,ided, and there was a large attend-- ance of volunteers. A lengthy programme of songs and recitations was gone through by various members of the company, and a very pleasant evening was Sr eat .

GOOD TESIPLARY. The Earlestown Ebenezer Lodge of Good Templars held its weekly session on Tuesday evening in the Co-operative Hall. Bro. A. Morris, C.T., presided, and there was a good attendance of members and visitors. One candidate was admitted to membership and several proposed arrangements were made for the forthcoming visit of Latchford Lodge on Tuesday evening next. The Programme for the evening was then gone into, sing- ing and readings being rendered by several of the mem- bers. The meeting was brought r,o a close in the usual way. WIIDDEN DEATH OP A WHERLWRIGET. A wheelwright named Shone, residing at 23, Warrington-road,Widnes,and who was well-known in Widnes and Runcorn, died suddenly on Sunday night. The deceased was partaking of supper when he suddenly lurched forward, and before as- sistance could lie rendered, he expired. He had not enjoyed good health for some time previously. For the past six weeks he had been employed by Mr. Thomas Dutton, of Burtonwood. SUDDEN DEATE AT EARLESTOWN. Thomas Wharten, green grocer, of 14, Queen-st., Earlestown, died very suddenly at his residence on Mon- day afternoon. The deceased who was 75 years of age, was breaking wood in the back yard, and going into the hcuse, complained to his wife that he had a pain in his left side and arm, and sat down in his chair, and his wife sent for Dr. Handyside and Mr. Peake, ckemist,but before medical aid arrived, he died, the medical opinion being that death was due to failure of the heart's action. Two years ago deceased suffered from epileptic fits. Success oF Locsr. STUDENTS.

Information has come to hand that Arthur J. Latham of tiaydock, has passed the minor examination of the Pharmaceutical Society, of Great Britain, at Edinburgh, early this month. H. J. Matthews passed the qualify- ing examination of the Pharmaceutical Society, and re- ceived ldi license to practice in Ireland. Richard Crossley has passed the examination nf the Royal College of Sur- geons,Eogland,and Royal College of Ph, sicians,;London. All the above commenced their training with Mr. Peake, chemist, Earlestown, and their successes must be gratifying to that gentleman. SILTING MATCH AT NRWTON.

Notwithstanding the thaw which set in on Sunday, a skating match took place on Newton lake on Monday afternoon, the ice being covered with water. The con- testants were Bate, ef Leigh, and Litherland of Sefton, the stakes being £25 a side. A very large number of People journeyed from Leigh and the neighbouring vil- lagea, and at the time advertised for the race, a respect- aisle crowd lined the banks of the lake. The course was 300 yards from scratch, Bate getting a flying start. Litherland made a desperate effort to overtake him, but when he appeared to be overhauling Bate, he unfortu- nately fell, and Bate sailed in an easy winner. GOOD TIMPLARS.
