Latchford, 11th Jan., 1895.

THE SEED QUESTION AND MR. PEERS' EXPERIMENT. .

Sir,—lt has been truly said that everybody has a special quality that stands out in front of all his other qualities, and is in a great measure the source of his in- dividuality. Our friend Mr. Peers' prominencv is invec- tive, and the edge of his satire is very keen. I was just thinking that had nature put him down as a lady wpat a bonny fish-wife she would have -made—a sort of orna- mental terror at Billingsgate. I am truly glad that my unhippy flounderings moved him to merriment and laughter. There is nothing that will please a weak intelleet so soon as irregular action. Dangle says that a good hearty laugh was dirt cheap at £lOO. Besides, Ido not know that this is not the first laugh that has ever been recorded at Penketh. lle might have sent me a couple of geese just as a small consideration for benefit received. I fear he never thought of it. besides he may be laughing yet. "One never knows." Or, he may be busy cutting up wheat— damping cloths and getting in a stock of sugar in pre-. paration for the ensuing harvest. Oh ! dear, what will the farmers say ? Mr. Peers has been talking about " light and more light on the seed question." Yet very few rays from him, come this way and the emanations from the" demonstration" are gone out into outer dark- ness, yet he wauld have us believe in his superiority. Then let him keep his gibes at Pl'llkPth and in their place put some little information. There are one or two points I want to clear up. First, his conceptions of the seed are erroneous. He says that the seed is composed of "a living part and a dead part." If we dissect a corn of wheat, these are exactly what we shall find. But, there is a some thing beyond our ken and that is the mysterious union of the two. 'This must not be broken ; if so, the Feed looses its nature and it ceases to he seed. Yet Mr. Peers tells us that he has done it successfully, which he has no right to say, because he had to substitute sugar for the part he surreptitiously removed. Nature has her own seed, her own land, and her own sowers ; by the wind, by birds, flies, aye• and by almost everything, seed is sown. When we see a little seed with its sails set scudding before the wind it is needless for Mr. Peers to run after it with a pinch of sugar as a offering,for every seed that requires sugar carries its own sugar maker. Seed then is indissoluble ; nature has made it so, and who shall say it nay. When Mr. Peers drinks water does he call it hydrogen and oxygen ? Certainly not, " if those two are put together in a certain proportion, and an electric spark is passed through them, they disappear and a quantity of wator, equal to weight in the sum of their weights, appears in their place " (Huxley). Be it observed these gases do not form water, it is the union that does the business. :eiature regards seed as units not as plurals. Mr. Peers in dividing the seed travelled a long way from nature. There is another part of this question expressed by Mr. Peers in these words: " Alas! for the dogma, of tile dying of the dead." This dogma did not come from me. In all that I have witten, I have been very care- ful not to write anything that wow ld lead to such an illogical conclusion, for I hold with Mr. Peers that it is the merest word' mongering to say that "when the living germ feeds up- on the non-living nutriment stored for its use, this non- living matter dies." To this I heartily subscribe. The next question is, when then or how does the seed die? Answer when it loses its life ! How is this brought about ? This is the way of it. 'lt is very well known that the young plants do not stay in the seed but grows nut of it, straight away carrying the life with it, just as in the case of an egg when the chick leaves it, there re- mains an empty shell. Before the seed is sown nature regards it as one, one small parcel of great possibilities lying dormant within its shelhpassive and inert. After sowing, all these are changed for energy, great strength and new forms—a new body successfully begun with the ruins of the old one. When these are exhausted the plant will be strong enough to make its own materials and to use them. Mr. Peers denies that the body of the seed is all used up in forming the foundation of the plant. lie says "it is all fiction ; facts say no !" We find that he put away the main body of the seed whilst the other part germinated on a damp cloth. And he -

SIR E. LEADER WILLIAMS AND TFIE CANAL. A HOPEFUL FORECAST. Sir E. Leader Williams, the engineer of the Manches- ter Ship Canal. on Siturday night delivered his in- augural address as President of the Manchester Associa- tion of Engineers at a meeting of the members at the Grand Hotel. In the course of his paper he said that owing to the sewage of Manchester' Salford, and all the other towns on the Rivers Irwell and Mersey and their tributaries being allowed to flow into the rivers without purification the state of the water in the canal had be- come very bad. Salford had now completed a system of intercepting sewers, and was about to purify the sewage at works connected at Mode Wheel. Sewage works are now being constructed by Manchester, and as the turnings of crude sewage into rivers or streams was contrary to the law as well as injurious to health, the other towns above Manchester must take imm- diate steps to prevent their sewage polluting the canal. Some of them had now got this neces- sary work in hand. His statement that steamers would be able to navigate day or night had proved correct even when vessels had not on beard the electric light, as there were electric lights at the docks and locks. He at first estimated ten hours as the time steamers would take to traverse the canal. That time was seldom taken, the passage generally being from seven to eight hours, passenger steamers even takinc, considerably less time Messrs. Burns' steamers work- ing' between Manchester ane Glasgow had averaged on 236 passages made during the year 1894 only 6 hours 33 minutes-117 passage: being made at night. He could see no reason why Lancashire, so famed for its machine shops, its boiler makers, and its artisans,could not establish on the banks of the Ship Canal works which should be able to compete with other districts for shipbuilding. Speaking on the prospect of the future, he pointed out that the result of the first year's work- ing had been to prove the efficient working of the canal and he had not lost faith in its future. It could not reasonably be expected that, amid the severe competi- tion that had to be encountered, even the energetic man- agement now dealing with traffic matters could all at once force from hostile or lukewarm traders the business which, nevertheless, must ultimately cOme to the canal, because no large waterway leading to a great manufac- • turing district could fail ultimately to obtain the traffic due to the advantages it could offer. STAII CLING COLLISION ON TuE CANAL NEAR, RUNCORN. Between nine and ten o'clock on Thursday morning the steamer Avalon, from Manchester, when proceeding along the Ship Canal towards Weston Point, collided with one of the dredging barges. The barge was laden with huge buckets Containing mud,and was in charge of two men named Charles Parry and Amos Atherton, of Runcorn. After being struck by the steamer the barge immediately capsized, throwing the men into the water, and sending the buckets with their contents to the bot- tom of the canal. With difficulty the men were rescued from their perilous position, but they are not much the worse for their mishap. There was no interference with traffic on the canal. Divers were soon afterwards set to work for the recovery of the buckets. THE POLLUTION OF HIE CANAL. ACTION AGAINS r SALFORD CORPORATION. The long-threatened action by the Mersey and Irwell Joint Committee against the Corporation of Salford for pouring sewage matter into the Ship Canal came on for hearieg in the Salford County Court on Thursday, before his Humour Judge Parry. Os behalf of the Joint Com- mittee it is sought to make out that although it appeared at one time that the Salford Corporation were disposed to do everything possible in the Way of purifying the effluent from their sewage works at Mode Wheel, they now occupied a very backward position in the matter, as they were only using the process of precipitation by lime,which is regarded as an entirely inefficient system unless it is acco mpanitd by filtration. The defence of the Corporation has not yet been stated, but it appears to he that all practicable and available means are being used to purify the effinent. A large number of wit- nesses are being called on beth sides. ANOTHER COLLIERY DISASTER.

GREAT LOSS OF LIFE. Although great progress was made with the relief operations at the Audley colliery on Wednesday, none of the entombed men have yet been rescued alive. In- deed, as the night wore on heavy falls and obstructious were discovered, which, it is feared, may postpone the discovery of the men for another twelve hours. Elope for those in the Bit. seam is wholly abandoned. It is known that they'are even now under water. Two dad bodies were discovered during 'Tuesday night and on Wednesday. An official list of those still in the pit puts the number at either seventy-five or seventy-eight. Of these thirty-five are married men and fifteen are lads. Fortunately all are members of the North Staffordshire Miners' Relief Fund. At midnight the men had been entombed sixty-six hours. At the Audley Colliery on Thursday, the search for the missing men was continued, but without any success. In the atternoon, owing to the presence of obstructions in the roadways, hope of rescuing any of the men alive was finally abandoned. The Coroner's inquest was opened on Thursday and adjourned.

The us ial weekly session of the Hope of Earlestown Lodge of tiood Tempters was held in the Co-operative Halton Monday eeening. Bro. Joseph Anderton, P.C., presided, and there was a large attendance of members. Two cardidates were admitted for membership, and several proposed. I report of No. 1 Convention was given by Bro. E. Newman C.T. and adopted. The pro- gramme of the evening was "Impromptu speeches," Bro. G. Clarke being responsible. A selection of appro. priate speeches had been provided, and they were well treated by the various naembers who gave addresses. EARLESTOWN DISTRICT SCHOOL. Mr. and Mrs. Brown, head master and mistress or the above school, gave their annual party to the teachers and a number of friends on Friday evening, in the schoolroom, Excellent arrangements had been made by the host ana hostess for the comfort of the guests, who numbered 45, and a most enjoyable time was spent. The schoolroom was decorated for the occasion, and pre- sente 1 a cheerful appearance. A full programme of dances was gone through, and at intervals several songs were given by members of the party. Mr. Peplow sup- plied the music,and as usual gave the greatest satisfac- tion in the selection he made, while Mr. R. Smith made aa efficient M.O. This gathering is always highly appreciated, and farms a pleasant break in the dull routine of school life. VIADUCT WORKS OFFICE STAFF DINNER. The fifth annual dinner of the office staff connected with the London and North-Western Company's Wagon Work at Eartestown, took place on Saturday evening I at the Bee Hotel, Liverpool. The officials, together with several ef the forem en, left Earlestown for Liver- I pool by the 1.34 train, and the afternoon was passed in various ways. Dinner was served soon after six o'clock, and was thoroughly enjoyed,the catering being in every I way efficient. After the good things had been disposed 1, of a social evening was spent, Mr. T. Ormand presiding,' and Mr. C. W. Emmett occupying the vice chair. The toasts of the " Queen," "The London and North- Western Railway Company Superintendent and Assis- tant," " The Foremen," " The Visitors," and " The Office," were proposed, and suitably responded to. Songs, Asc., were given during the evening by Messrs. E. Swann, C. H. Lea, C. W. Emmett, J. Swann, J. Finch, and H. Tarbuck, a very harmonious evening being brought to a close shortly before eleven o'clock. BURTONWOOD SCHOOL BOARD. The usual monthly meeting of the above Board was held in the classroom of the Board Schools, Burtonwood, on Thursday evening, when there were present Mr. Jas. Warburton (chairman), Mr. Andrew Jackson (vice- chairibran),Rev.A.M. Mitchell and ELGawthorpe (school master), Mr. F. Wright (attendance officer), and Mr. Wm. Webster (clerk). The minutes of the previous meeting were read and confirmed and the correspond- ence of the month considered. The Treasurer's book was examined and the sums of £llO for rate and £l9 15s. fee grant were ordered to be debited in the cash hook. The attendance was discussed and the Clerk I was ordered to procure certain forms for the attendance ' officer's use. The Clerk was directed to purchase a lamp for the schoolroom. It was resolved not to meet , again until the 7th of March, and a cheque for I current salaries having been signed the Board rose at 8.30 p.m. SONS OF TEMPERANCE. On Wednesday evening the usual meeting of the Samuel Morley Section of Cadets of Temperance was held in the Co-operative Hall, Eirlestown. Ern. W. Gibbs, W.A., preside,d. After opening, five new naem bees were initiated into the Order, 45 were present. The Worthy Patron reported that he had visited the Earl of Shaftsbury Section, St. Helens, and that they had doubled tho numbers in three months. Ihe Britain's Need Division ,met in the same room at eight o'clock. Bro. S Foden, W.T., presided. After the opening and other business was disposed of a very profitable and pleasant evening was spent, the subject being " Impromptu speeches." Bro. John Pickett was responsible. No member was allowed to speak more than five minutes, questions were written on slips of paper and placed in a hat. Each member drew one out, such as " Wily am I a teetotaller ?" " Does the bible sanction the use of intoxicating drink ?" " Are pithlicans suitable persons for poor-law guardians ?' " Ought all preachers of the gospel to be teetotalers." At the close of all questions discussions followed, and a very profitable meeting was closed. CHRISTIAN MEETING ROOM, EARLESTOWN. An excellent paper was given at the Improvement 'Class on Tuesday evening by Mr. A. Holdsworth, on " Music in wership, its place and power in the church." Mr. B. Harrison presided, and the attendance was good. The essayist showed how the music was appre- ciated amongst the ancient Egyptians,Assyrians,Greeks, Etruscans, Romans, Hindoog, and Chinese in their various forms of worship ; but his main purpose was to show the uses of music in the church from a New 'estament standpoint. Be said it might be freely as- serted that music in worship affects not God, but our- selves. The power of music, its subtle influence, its pathos,its exhilarating inelody,its transcendent adapti- bility to every passing wave of emotion ever felt in the human breast, all combine to render it a vehicle for in- tercommunion between man and his maker. It often happened—which was much to be deplored—that in- stead of being usi d to ennoble and uplift, to softentand subdue, to chasten and inspire, it was used merely to attract. In the hands of a godless and worldly minded choir it often ministered to vanity and pride, it usurped the place of true devotion and created a demand for an altogether less ennobling style of sanctuary music, which acta and reacts upon both congregation and choir until it a tiling did not " go well," the most sub- lime thoughts poet ever penned, counted for nothing. A ' most lively and useful discussion, which, was taken up ' by many of the members, brought the meeting to a close. In these times of agri-ultural depression when S n •erowing, is such a losing game the farmer will un- doubtedly turn his attention to the breeding and rearine; of live stock, and if judiciously managed with tie ad of recent discoveries and investigation it is bound to be p ofitable. The prccess of cheese and butter tusking, is greatly improves upon, and there is not the loss in the infant stage of youeg animals that was once the case, nor loss or injury through difficult parturition, colic, chills, or accioents since the introduction of Day Son and Hewitt's calving and lambing chests, which hould be in every farmstead. They seem to bear the tamp, if not of the infallible, the nearest approach to that medical science has yet reached.

Sill? CANAL.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

FOOTBALL. IRICIEBTERS should acquire the "Pocket Diary and Companion issued by the well-known Sports Manu- factures, George Bussey I Co., Peckham, London. All the Information Cricketers require is found therein, as well as the usual Diary, yet it will go in the waistcoat pocket. Leather bound, gilt edges, 6d., postage id. If you are not satisfied U. G. B. Si Co. will return the price. The weather on Saturday was again of a severe character, and consequently football was Out of the question. Warrington were due at Rochdale to play the St. Clement's club, but the match was cancelled in con- sequence of the frost, as also was the match between the A teams. Every match in the Warrington district as also cancelled, and the first match since this year came in has yet to be played.

The frost has had a rare innings indeed, to the great delight of the skaters, and skate makers and sellers,who have been doing a " roaring " trade lately. However, the frost has gone away, for a time at any rate, and the old fashioned muddy weather has come again. This is not a very weleome change, for no one likes to go tramping through the sloppy streets, and having their nether extremities covered with mud. The frost re- returned yesterday, but not to a very great extent.

Muddy weather, however, does not interfere with football matches to the same extent as frost, and there is, therefore, every probability that the match with Salford will take place. A renewed acquaintance with the lately suspended club will therefore be made. The "A "team will have a stiff fight with Salford " A" at Warrington, and will have to put their best foot first to , come out victorious.

To meet the Salford team at Salford, the following team has been selected to do duty for Warrington : Full back, J. Boscow ; three-quarters, Barber, Moores, Fleesom, and Berton; half backs, Bate and Nevins; forwards. Turner Jolley, Dakm, Taylor, Houghton, Savage, Bailey, and Barrett.

Affairs in the Football Association League are becom- ing interesting an there will be a rare fight for the championship between Sunderland, Aston Villa, and Everton, and it would be very difficult to pick the winner out of these three There are circumstances which favour all three. Aston Villa, for instance, have four matches to play at home, and only two away, but as they are at present in a relatively worse position than either If verton or Sunderland, they will have to trust to good fortune a little, and hope that some of the other chips in the League wi/l do them a good turn by beating the above mentioned clubs.

Sunderland have only three matches to play at home, against six away, se that if they are to regain the cham- pionship, they will have to fight hard. Everton have five matches at hone and four matches away, but among their • 'away" engagements are numbered Sunderland and Aston Villa, where they are almost cer- tain of a downtall. Therefore considering the chances of the three clubs, it is a difficult problem to pick out the club which has really the best chance.

The bottom three are even still more difficult to ch oose, but they will most probably be Stoke, Derby Co. and Wolverhampton Wanderers. The " Wolves 'at present have a pretty respectable positi on upon the League table, but this is only apparent when the fact is mentioned that they have only ene match left at horse. As they have not won one match away, and only drawn one, it is not very probable that they will have many more victories this season. In fact judging the matter in this light they hold the very worse position on the table.

In the second League, Bury was almost a certainty for the position of champions,and Grimsby Town and New- ton Beath are apparently going strong for the secoad and third positions. Notts County,and Darwin however may have something to say on the *latter yet. Bury has very slight cause to fear for they have obtained such a commanding lead.

Leaving the Association league, and coming to the Lancashire Rugby League, I see that the two leaders, Oldham and Tyldesley meet to-day, and a splendid fight may be looked forward to. The Tyldesley men are very confident of winning the match, and there is no doubt but that Oldham have a task before them. The Tyldesley forwards should in fast win the match for the " Bongers." The other match in the competition is that between Broughton Rangers and Rochdale Hornets, and , s well contested game should result.

===== 0002601_18950119_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrington and Salford also meet,but as Salford have bees expunged frem the League, the match will only be classed as an ordinary one. The interest in the League has indeed almost evaporated,owing to the three clubs, Leigh, Wigan, and Salford being put outside its pale this season. There cannot be very much enthu- slate when only seven clubs are engaged. Indeed, ten is rather a small number, and there ought at least to be twelve clubs in. It would be a good plan next season, for the committee of the League to reinstate the expelled clubs, and also take in the two clubs of the second division who have gained the highest number of points in the s c md division without having to fight for it. 01 course the other two divisions could be likewise lc er asea.

In the Lancashire Amateur Cup Competition the old Amp efordians have scratched to Warrington in the first romid,and Warrington consequently entered the second rousti unopposed.

PENIZETH.

OLD FOLK'S TREIT.—The 9th annual treat for the old folks of Penketh and surrounding districts was held on Monday night last in the Conservative Club. The state of the roads was very bad, but this did not keep the old p-ople away from the long expected treat. Over 70 people sat down to a very substantial tea, which was under the superintendence of Mesdames Routledge, Longshaw, Pownall, J. Routledge and the Misses M. and C. Foster. After the old people had partaken of a hearty tea, the stage was cleared, and Mr. J. C. Rout- lodge, took the chair. At the commencement of the proceedings Mr. Routledge remarked how pleased he was to see such n o,oedly gathering present, and hoped that they would enjoy themselves to the utmost. He spoke very feelingly of the death of their friend and 'supporter Mr. R. Da riiman. Mr. Lee was then called on for a song " Fat I,:r 0 Flynn " which was given in excellent style. Miss Leatherbarrow of Widnes then sang " This and That " very prettily, and was Followed by Mr. Harry Mainley with " The 7th Royal Fusiliers." Mr. W. Picton gave the company a rare treat with his two accordion solos " Ye Banks arid Brass," and "The Last Rose of Sum- mer." " Annie Laurie," was very sweetly rendered by Miss Railton, and Mr. George Neil, of Widnes, was heartily encored for his amg, " Eight Hours a Day." Mr. Walters, without whom the meeting would seem strange, sang his time-honoured song, " What will be- come of boa." This was followed by a duet by Mr. J. Lee and Miss Leatherbarrow, "The Music Lesson." it musical sketch by the Brothers Foster, " The Upper Ten," received a loud encore. Miss Railton sang, " Gin a Body," and was followed by Mr. Neil with "The Lodger," who was loudly encored, establishing himself a prime favourite with the old folks. Mr. C. Brown accompanied the artibtes on the pianoforte. During the entertainment, refreshments were lavishly supplied at the tables, andj the men were given churchwardens and tobacco, and puffed away with great satisfaction. At the conclusion of the meeting a ilb. of tea was pre- sented to 'the women, and the men each received lox. of tobacco. The Committee of Management were : Messrs. J. C. Routledge (chairman), Jonas Foster (sec.), Mainley. Cross, Denton, Longshaw, Pownall, Walters, and J. Jackson. A 'bus was in waiting to carry home the long-distance visitors. The expense was borne by subscription. The committee are to be congratulated on the success of toe meeting, which, to quote Mr. J. C. Routledge, is " The best we've ever had. The meeting ended with the strains of "Auld Lang Sync" Cuonsa, SOCIETY.—On Monday evening the members of the above society recommenced their practices in the Wesleyan Schools, and are preparing for a concert which will be given in Easter week. Several new mem- bers were:enrolled. The society is in a flourishing con- dition. LORD RANDOLPEI CHURCHILL.

A HOPELASS )31.1LLETIN. Sir Russell Reynolds was yesterday afternoon called in to consult with the other doctors concerning the condition of Lord Randolph Churchill, with the result that the following bulletin signed by four medical men was issued :—" Lord Randolph Churchill is in an ad- vanced stsge of general paralysis, and although his heart's action is a little improved to-day any change for the better in his condition can be expected to be only slight and temporary."

It is stated that the Goverment has promised to in- troduce the amended Friendly Societies Bill as a Govern- ment measure during the ensuing session of Parlia- ment. , ,

AN ENGLISHMAN'S :HOUSE IS HIS CASTLE or, properly speaking, ought to be. ' . • Never make the raistake.of supposing that it will be "time enough" when the second teeth come in to teach the child how to •cleanse the teeth. This habit is not easily instilled later if not begun in early childhood. I know one young lady who first wielded a tooth-brush at the age of two years. She is about three and a-half now, cleanses her teeth herself, and they are as clean as her mother's, which is saying much. The father is a dentist, which may have something to do a ith the exceptional state of affairs. Teach the child to clean the teeth, and see that it is done theroughty night and morning. Even the temporary set should be preserved, if fok no other reason than because'sorne Of them will not be lOst until the t -smith year, and therefore must be retained among the new teeth. If they are decayed they will conta- minate the others, because decay is contagious, especi- ally in the same mouth. 'there are many kinds of stains ,which occur on teeth, none of which really injure the tooth itself with a single exception. Green stain is a dangerous foe to the enamel. Unlike other stains, it appears most frequently on the upper six front teeth. In appearance it is a greenish slime,resembling the scum seen on stagnant pools of water. ' If cleaned off the enamel is found chalky in colour and existence, and is readily chipped away so that a filling is nee led. What is the cause of this has not been actually demon- atrated. _
